mouth. THERE is but one perfect man—her first hus- band. THERE is nothing one can get accustomed to quicker than luxury. "Is the medicine hard to take?" "No. Just as easy as the cold was." possible anonymous subscribeis. WITH most of us it is up to-day and down to- morrow, and the day after and many succeeding days. THIS is "the theory and practice of medicine." CAPITA.L"

Mr. Pulitzer, the proprietor of the "New York World," who has so greatly distinguished himself by advocating peace in the teeth of the general agitation against England, is a frequent visitor to London. For years he has invariably employed some Englishman of good birth as his companion and private secretary. Mr. Pulitzer is a remark- able man, and as a journalist scarcely has his equal. Some years ag ), before the last nomination for the Presidency of the United States toe& place, he was at Nice. Probably all his correspondents at home, it is said, were hostile to the nomination of Mr. Cleveland. Mr. Pulitzer is almost blind, and has, therefore, to rely considerably upon others for in- formation. Notwithstanding this, and notwith- standing that his own city and State had declared against the nomination of the present President,he at once telegraphed to the editor ordering that the "New York World " should support the nomination of Mr. Cleveland. The " Wcrld " was the cnly paper on the Democratic side which did so ; but events proved how correct was the judgment of Mr. Pulitzer, for his candidate was elected.

Since the successful ;inauguration of the Man- chester Ship Canal the interest of the engineering world has been largely fixed upon another great enterprise which is now approaching completion. Manchester engineers had to solve the problem of carrying their sea traffic overland. The London engineers have been engaged on the almost equally difficult problem how to carry their land traffic under water. The Blackwell Tunnel under the Thames below Greenwich is the result, and on Saturday Mr. A. R. Binnie, the chief engineer of the London County Council, celebrated the suc3ess- ful accomplishment of the more exceptional part of the work by entertaining a large party of en- gineers and other experts below the bed of the river. Tunnelling is of course now a simple matter, but the unique features of this scheme are that the tunnel has been made through loose and shifting gravel, full of water, within a few feet of the actual river,and practically, 80 far as the difficulties to be overcome were concerned, almost through the water itself.. The task of removing the gravel and building the tunnel has only been accomplished by working in compressed air under pressure equal to that of the water outside. This has involved con- siderable danger and the invention of many novel engineering devices, and the successful construc- tion ot that part of the tunnel which is under the river without any hitch or disaster, is a great triumph for all concerned. The great steel cham- ber or " shield," weighing 250 tons, in which the work has been done has now been forced right under the river from side to sipe, leaving a megni- ficently constructed roadway and footpaths 22ft. wide behind it. It is now under the wharves of the northern bank, and will very shortly emerge in the deep cutting which has already been made as an approach to the tunnel when completed.

There can be no doubt that to the man in the street, Mr. Chamberlain has been the hero of the hour through the Transvaal crisis. In all circles on Sunday his action came in for nothing but eulogy. There was never any strong sympathy with Dr. Jameson's action, but on Saturday and Sunday even the slight feeling for him,based purely on personal considerations, had practically dis- appeared now that the extreme gravity of his action had become clear. What particularly elicited admiration was the story of Mr. Chamberlain's "three o'clock in the morning courage "—namely, his visit to the office of the EasternTelegragh Com- pany in order to examine into the causes of the stoppage of telegrams. Though he had been in ill- health during a considerall) period of the crisis, and though his work has been practically continii- ous day and night, he turned up at the Colonial Office on the morning of his lonely journey to the City cool, clear, and collected, without the appear- ance,of having turned a hair.

President Kruger's reply to the German Emperor and the tone adopted towards the Kaiser's tekgram by foreign Powers are doing much to temper the anger of the British public at the Emperor's insult. It may or may not be true that the Queen has made no secret of the distress her grandson's impetuosity has caused her, but there are ominous mutterings among Englishmen that this ungracious foe is an admiral of the British Navy and colonel of one of nur crack cavalry regiments—the Ist Royal Dragoons. Perhaps it is well that Parliament is not sitting, for reference to such things would surely leak out, together with renewed observations on the Duke of Coburg's position as owning fealty to the Emperor. The German Ambassador sought an interview with Lord Salisbury on Monday, but the Premier was at Hatfield.

It becomes increasingly apparent that the Govern- ment authorities in .Ottawa, intend to go in for a strong policy of defence, and that this policy will probably be endorsed by their successors after this year's general elections. According to a COMMIIni • ciation which has been received from a high official in Ottawa, not only is the militia to be greatly strengthened both as regards numbers and areas, but a big plan is aso projected in the way of a line of defences connected by railways right acr)ss the continent. entiferences on the latter subject will shortly be held in London. The question of the fast mail service will also be thoroughly discussed in time, it is hoped, for something definite to be done by the Imperial Government during the forthcona- ing session.

Lord Salisbury is quite well Aware. of the im- mense amount of dissatisfaecion which prevails among a large section or the Conservatives—the older school of them, who were born into, and in- herited all tho traditions of the policy of the Crimean War—at his policy of coercing the Sultan. Tl_has,hirnself, had to undergo a considerable amount of evolution to arrive at the stage to which, diplomatically, he has recently attained ; but the older members of his party, who are not the victims of diplomatic necessity, and he is, re- tain the crust of their Toryism perfectly unbroken. They detest the persistency and energy of Canon M'Coll in the crusade which he carries on against Turkey, and in the Tory clubs the canon does not receive many blessings. The massacres and atroci- ties committed on the Armenia population shock and horrify a Liberal ; hut the old dread of Russia and the old fetish of Con,tantinople are still such strong elements in their political creed that they hold that anything which weakens the Ottoman F.mpire is simply playing into the hand q of the Northern Power. Of course, they are too loyal to their party flag to remonstrate to the Government, but not a few of them rejoice that events elsewhere have released Lord Salisbury from the pressure which had carried him and his policy far beyond the traditional limits.

An exhibition meagre in extent and of choice quality in only a few classes was opened on Tues- day at the Crystal Palace,under the auspices of the National Peristeronic Society. About 400 birds were staged. Only 30 members of the Society had contributed. This is a pity, because tbe Society.now about 35 years old, has in times pas?, done much to improve the breed of pigeons, and has In many in- starves given the lead to " the ;fancy." Unless the members are prepared to make a better show ano- ther year the event had better be taken out of the calendar. The annual exhibiticn is not competi- tive, and consequently no prizes are given. The best birds shown on Tuesday wßre jleobins,dragons, magpies, and orientals. Several ot these had taken prizes elsewhere, and were vary fine specimens. Other varieties once so much fancied appeared to , be falling off in point of quality.

It was once widely expected that the New Year would bring the announcement of Sir Julian Gold- smid's elevation to the peerage. Instead, we have news that the illness which is supposed to have led him to decline the contemplated honour has had a fatal ending. There were few,/ men in the House of Commons more highly trusted than Sir Julian. Be was the embodiment of intlxibla honour, and, although somewhat peremptory when discharging the duties of Deputy-Chairman of Committee, in which he greatly delighted, his fairness was never questioned, while his rapid grasp of the business afGot was often admired. He was a serious loss to the Liberals when, at the great parting of the ways in 1886, he cast in his lot with the Unionists, and he is no less a loss to the Unionists now. Sir Julian was one of the most enthusiastic chess- players in the Flouse of Commons. I remember on one occasion seeing him enjoying a game in the smoking-room with an bon. member he had only half an hour before threatered to name to the lieuse. He was personally very genial, but for years he had been a constant sufferer from gout and kindred ailments.

LADIES' LETTER

Mt DEARTST NELLIE,

You want some hints, of course, about ball. dresses. It seems to me they are particularly light and graceful this winter, and the prettiest I have seen were also the simplest. A most effective design is one which has the skirt and bodice of pale green satin, veiled with fine white net, this having a ruche of the same at the hem, and a narrow band of silver and pearl galon at the knee. The square neck had a garniture of pink roses, and the sleeves were of rose-leaf petal satin, with straps of the galon. Ten yards of green satin, six of double width net, and two and a-half of pink satin will be sufficient. Of course, if a very cheap dress is required, the foundation may be of a good sateen, but really when nice satin is obtainable for is. na. the yard, it cannot be termed expensive. In either ease the skirt should be lined with white linenette. The net overskirt is quite loose from this slip, or, rather, it is attached at the waist only. The same design looks well also with black net, and this is especially suitable for Cinderellas, as it can be worn over different-coloured foundations, and

trimmed with different ribbons from time. Another pretty gown is entirely made of silver-grey tulle over white satin, with white ostrich plumes on the shoulders and white satin bows and ceintures. A smart and attractive coat is made of seal brown plush, trimmed with handsome silk orna- ments, and lined throughout with quilted satin to match. The fronts are cut in one with the first sidepiece, and are fitted to the figure by a single bust dart each side. The second sidepiece and back are well shaped to set out in full flutes to the lower edge. It fastens in the centre of front in- visibly with hooks, being trimmed each side from the shoulder with silk cord ornaments, which terminate just below the bust. The neck is com- pleted with a collar-band and turn-down collar of double plush, interlined with buckram. The bishop sleeves are lined throughout and gathered between the upper edge of wristband, and the ful- ness at the top is gathered into the armhole. Velveteen, too, plays a large part in fashionable coats. A very rich-looking velveteen coat was worn the other day at a reception after a wedding by a lady who had been unable to venture out of the house in a dominating colour. The revers, cuffs, and pockets were of chestnut brown velvet,

and down the centre of the front there was a box pleat of brown satin to match, and a jabot of tea- tinted lace. The two buttons at the waist were curious old miniatures, and those on the pockets and down the front were moth.n antiques, but harmonised extremely well. A useful gift for this time of the year is a travel- ling set, consisting of glove, handkerchief, and night-dress cases, a soiled linen bag, and a trunk cover, all to match. Orange linen, worked with a very large single initial in black flax thread, looks uncommon, but blue or apricot linen, embroidered in white, is perhaps prettier, and washes better. There shoula be no wadding in either glove or handker.nief case, on account of the space it occupies, and the night-dress sachet should be of the modern large shape, with a big square flap. The trunk cover alluded to is merely a square of linen used to tuck over the contents of a travelling trunk. All the cases, by the way, should be nicely perfumed by sprinkling a pinch or so of sachet powder beneath the lining. Instead of embroidery in flax or silk an appliqué of linen in a contrasting colour to the ground is a pretty way of ornament- ing handkerchief and glove sachets, &c., and a simple design, such as stars or detached leaves, looks well if neatly cut out and carefully sewn on, the stitches being concealed by feather-stitching, herring-boning, or narrow washing braid. Muffs are still of all materials, as you know. Furs are very popular, as they have always been,

but one of the smartest materials is black plush. I saw one quite recently in this material, and it was trimmed with feather tips and satin ribbon. In arranging table decorations there is nothing so appropriate to Vie time of year, of course, as holly. Owing to its stiff and prickly nature, it is most difficult to arrange, but the result is good, when carried out properly. For example, a ball of holly, as a centre-piece, in the middle a bit of mistletoe, and on this a small Cupid with glittering wings; up one side of the holly ball runs a ladder of scarlet berries, and on the top of the ladder and half way up are two more Cupids. Sprays of holly are on the cloth—these to be judiciously sprinkled with frost. A green or crimson plush centre cloth, bordered with holy, gives a warm and cheerful appearance. Little bowls of holly dispersed over the table, with a small Cupid on a branch in each bowl, adds to the general bright effect. For a supper table at a Christmas party "Father Christ. mas ' should be the centre-piece. He can stand on a snow bank, which would extend nearly the length of the table. Dishes of fruits, cakes, and crackers, decorated, and arranged with holly and flags, well frosted; long sprays of holly from the bank, and at each end of the table sprays of green foliage, tied with large bows of bright ribbon, with a small bunch of holly in each bow. Your loving JENNIZ.

Sir Robert Ball thinks the time is approaching when posterity will be able to construct machinery that will be worked by means of glasses, with beat obtained by the direct action of the sun's rays. A window tax was introduced by William 111. in 1695, iecreased by the Georges and repealed in 1851. Rouses of eight windows weretaxed 16s. : of ten, £1 Bs. ; of 100, £29 Bs. Were it not for the 'multitude of storks that throng to Ezy pt every winter there would be no living in the country, for after every inundation frogs appear in most incredible numbers. The blood flows almost as freely through the bones as through the flesh of very young children, but as the age comes on the blood vessel in the bones are almest filled by the deposition of matter. Several cities of ancient Greece levied a tax on bachelors over 20 years old, to compel them to marry. The tax was so large that matrimony was cheaper by comparison. The use of forks at a meal was known among us some centuries ago, but the custom was for long looked upon as foppish, and did not become widespread until the end of the last and beginning of the present century. The German Emperor and Empress are very generous to their dependents—so generous, indeed, to their chil- dren's English nurse that she sometimes complains that the beautiful dresses and jewellery they bestow upon her are too gorgeous for her ever to wear. The Princess of Wales has, it is said, recently sent a special and highly-prized token of her esteem to Sir Frederic Leighton, the recipient of one of the new Year's honours. The gift is an arm-chair covered with art needlework, wrought entirely by the Princess's own hand. Is is well known that sugar and alcohol can be extracted from wood. But it appears that better results may be squeezed out of peat. A certain chemist has found that by judicious treatment one thousand pounds of peat can be made to yield as much alcohol as can be obtained from five hundred pounds of potatoes. The peach is said to have been originally a poisonous almond. Its fruity parts were used to poison arrows, and for that purpose were introduced into Persia. Transplantation has not only removed its poisonous qualities, but turned it into the delicious fruit we now enjoy. "'I he great fault with home-made millinery," eaid a woman who had learned the business, "is that the amateur puts in too many stitches. Why, I often merely pin the trimming on my bat and wear it that way for weeks." The same caution applies to fancy work. Take long stitches on the underside, and do not fasten bows too tightly.

Quito the Most Ghastly Brooch I know of, says a jeweller, "is made of an eye—and a human eye at that. It—the pm. I mean—belongs to a London lady, and the eye which forms the centre of the brooch was removed a year ago. Her husband, who lost it on account of an accident while shooting, jokes about it in the most unconcerned fashion, but to many it is a most unpleasant sight. It is slightly outre if nothing more.

Royalty and Marriage. Apropos of Princess Maud' s marriage, it has been noticed that the Royal Princesses have nearly all married at an earlier age than their brothers. The Princess Royal was only eighteen, the Princess Alice nineteen,and the Princess Helena twenty at the time of their marriage. The Princess Louise was twenty-three, and the Princess Beatrice twenty-eight, which gives an average for the five of about twenty-two. When the Princess Louise of Wales was twenty-two she married the Duke of File,and Prince; Maud is now not quite twenty-six. On the other hand, the average age of the Queen's sons on their marriage was between twenty seven and twenty-eight. The Prince of Wales was in his twenty-second year, but his three brothers were twenty-nine or more before they became Benedicts, and the Duke of York was twenty-eight.

Paying, But Monotonous. It must be a somewhat monotonous way for a girl to earn her living by exhibiting her long hair as an ad- vertisement. According to an interview in " Woman's Life," they may not turn their faces half round uhder any circumstances, which is a hard thing to do in the face of the frank and free criticisms they have to listen ti, and which is ale-) the chief thing that makes the occupation distasteful. The most disparaging remarks come from women ; the most galling ones from men. The latter make all sorts of practical jokes to make the girls turn their heads. A well-known clubman and army officer lately opened the door suddenly whilst his man threw a couple of mice amongst the girls. His credit as a gentleman was kept up by giving each girl a present to atrne for the fright. The compensating advantages are ti at tie pay is good, and that there are few who have sufficiently luxuriant hair to act as an advertise ment. Another fact is that the greater number of those employed from time to time have been young married women, and most of the girls who were not have found lovers actually as a consequence of their employment, and have married very well indeed in some cases. It is one of the difficulties of the management to keep girls, so readily do they find husbands.

The Late Supper. The old tradition that to eat anything; just before go- ing to bed was sure to produce indigestion and render sleep impossible, is now happily exploded. It is not good as a matter of fact, to go to the bed with the stomach so loaded that the undigested food will render one restles s but something of a light,palatable nature in the stormich is one of the best aids to quietude and rest. The process of digestion goes on in sleep with as much regularity as when one is taking violont exercise to aid it, and some- thing iR the stomach is a very desirable condition for a night's rest. Indeed, is has recently been pointed out, that a good deal of the prevalent insomnia is the result of an unconscious craving of the stomach for food in persons who have been unduly frightened by the opinion that they must not eat before going to bed, or who have like many nervous women, been keeping themselves in state of semi-starvation. Nothing is more agreeable on retiring for the night than to take a bowl of hot broth, or oatmeal gruel, or chocolate. It is a positive aid to nervous people, and induces peaceful slumber. This is especially the case on cold winter nights, when the stomach craves warmth as much as any other part of the body. Even a glass of hot milk is grateful to the palate on such occasions, but a light, well cooked gruel is better, and in our climate, during the cold months of winter, it should be the retiring food.

THE HIDDEN HAND;

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY lis. E. D. E. N. SOUTHWORT H.

CHAPTER XVIII. THE D,WTOR'S DAUGHTER. " Oh, b Alle, it seemed half holy, As if drawn from thoughts more far, Than our common jestings are. And if any painter drew her, He would paint her unaware With a halo sound her hair:"

—E. B. BROWNING. On the appointed day, Traverse took his way to Willow Heights, to keep his tryst and enter upon the medical studies in the good doctor's office. He was anxious also to know if his patron had as yet thought of any plan by which his mother might better her condition. He was met at the door by little Mattie, the parlour maid, who told him to walk right up stairs into the study, where his master was expecting him. Traverse went up quietly and opened the door of that pleasant study-room, to which the reader has already been introduced, and the windows of which opened upon the upper front piazza. Now, however, as it was quite cold, the windows were down, though the blinds were open, and through them streamed the golden rays of the morning sun that fell glistening upon the fairy hair and white raiment of a young girl, who sat reading before the fire. The doctor was not in the room, and Traverse in his native modesty was just about to retreat, when the young creature looked up from her book, and seeing him, arose with a smile, and came forward. saying: "You are the young man whom any father was expecting, I presume. Sit down, he has stepped out, but will be in again very soon." Now, Traverse being unaccustomed to the society of young ladies, felt excessively bashful when suddenly coming into the presence of this refined and lovely girl. With a low bow and a deep blush he took the chair she placed for him. With natural politeness, she closed her book and addressed herself to entertaining him. "I have heard that your mother is an Invalid; I hope she is better ? " "I thank you—yes, ma'am— Miss," stammered Traverse, in painful embarrassment. Understanding the timidity of the bashful boy, and seeing that her efforts to entertain only troubled him, she placed the newspapers on the table before him, saying: "Here are the morning journals if you would like to looking over them, Mr. Rocke," and then she resumed her book. " I thank you, Miss," replied the youth, taking up a paper, more for the purpose of covering his embarrassment, than for any other. Mr. Rocke 1 Traverse was seventeen years of age, and had never been called Mr. Rocke before! This young girl was the very first to compliment him with the manly title, and he felt a boyish gratitude to her and a harmless wish that his well-brushed Sunday suit of black was not quite so rusty and threadbare, tempered by an innocent exultation in the thought that no gentleman in the land could exhibit fresher linee, brighter shoes or cleaner hands than himself. But not many seconds were spent in such egotism. He stole a glance at his lovely com- panion sitting on the opposite side of the fire-side —he was glad to see that she was already deeply engaged in reading, for it enabled him to observe her, without embarrassment or offence. He had scarcely dared to look at her before, and had no distinct idea of her beauty. There had been for him only a vague, dazzling vision of a golden-haired girl in floating white raiment, waf tin the fragrance of violets as she moved, and with a vo.ce sweeter than the notes of the cushat dove as she spoke. Now he saw that the golden hair flowed in ring- lets around a fair, roseate face, soft and bright with feeling and intelli;ence. As her dark blue eyes followed the pige, a smile intense with mean:ng deepened the expression of her countenaece. That intense smile I—it was like her father's, only lovelier —more heavenly. That intense smile 1 It had, even on the old doctor's face, an inexpressible charm for Traverse—but on the lovely young face of his daughter it exercised an ineffable fascination. So earnest and so unconscious became the gaze of poor Traverse that he was only brought to a sense of propriety by the opening of the door, and the entrance of the doctor, 3 exclaimed: " Ah 1 here already, Traverse I that is punctual ! This is my daughter Clara, Traverse! Clara, this is Traverse, you've heard me speak about I—But, I dare say, you've already become acquainted," con- cluded the doctor, drawing his chair up to the read- ing-table, sitting down and folding his dressing- gown around his limbs. "Well, Traverse, how is the little mother 7" he presently inquired. "I was just telling Miss Day that she was much better, sir," said Traverse. " Ab, ha ah, ha 1" muttered the doctor to himself —" that's kitchen physic—roast turkey and port wine; and moral medicine, hope I—and mental medicine, sympathy." "Well, Traverse," he said aloud, "I have been racking my brain for a plan for your mother—and to no purpo::e I Traverse, your mother should be in a home of peace, plenty and cheerfulness 1— I can speak before my little Clara here I never have have any sect ets from her—Your mother wants good living, cheerful company, and freedom from toil and care! The situation of gentleman's or lady's house- keeper in some home of abundance, where she would be esteemed as a member of the family would suit her; but where to find such a place 1 I have been inquiring, without mentioning her name, of course —among all roy friends, but not one of them wants a houbekeeper, or knows a soul who does want one, and so I am at sea on the subject.' I'm ashamed of myself for not succeeding better 1" "Oh, sir, do not do yourself so great injustice," said Traverse. "Well, the fact is after boasting BO confidently that I would find a good situation for Mrs. ltocke, lo and behold I have proved myself as yet only a boaster! "Father," said Clara, turning upon him her sweet eyes. "Well, my love 7" "Perhaps Mrs. Backe would do us the favour to co me here and take charge of our household." "Eli I what! I never thought of that! I never had a housekeeper in my life ? " exclaimed the doctor. "No, sir, because you never needed one before, but now we really do. Aunt Mogey has been a very faithful and efficient manager, although she is a coloured woman; but she is getting very old." "Yes, and deaf, and blind, and careless! I know she is! I have no doubt in the world she scours the coppers with the table napkins, and washes her face and hands in the soup tureen." " father l'' said Clara. "Well, Clara, at least she wants looking after." "Father, she wants rest in her sld age." "No doubt of it! no doubt of it 1" " And, father, I intend, of course, in time, to be Jour housekeeper; but having spent all my life at a boarding-sohool, I know very little about domestic affairs, and I require a great deal of instruction; so I really do think that there is no one needs Mrs. Rocke's assistance more than we do, and if she will do us the favour to come, we cannot do better than engage her." "To be sure to be sure I Lord bless my soul! to think it should never have entered my stupid old head, until it was put there by Clare! Here was I searching blindly all over the country for a situation for Mrs. Rocke, and wanting her all the time more than any one else! That's the way, Traverse, that's the way with us all, my boy! While we are looking away off yonder for the solution of our difficulties, the remedy is all the time lying just under our noses 1" "But so close to our eyes, father, that we cannot see it! said Clara. "Jest so, Clare I just so! You are always ahead of me in ideas! Now, Traverse, when you go home this evening you shall take a note to your mother, setting forth our wishes—mine and Clara's ; if she accedes to them she will make us very happy." With a great deal of manly strength of mind Traverse had all his mother's tenderness of heart. It was with difficulty that he could keep back his tears or control his voice, while he answered: "I remember reading, sir, that the young queen of England when she came to her throne wished to provide handsomely for an orphan companion of her childhood; and seeing that no office in her house- hold suited the young person, she created one for her benefit. Sir, I believe you have made one for my mother." "Not at all! not at all If she doesn't come to look after our housekeeping, old Mog,gy will be greasing our griddles with tallow candle ends next! If you don't believe ma, ask Clara! ask Clara I" Not "believe" him If the doctor had affirmed that the moon was made of mouldy cheese, Traverse would have deemed it his duty to stoutly maintain that astronomical tl—ory._ He felt hurt that the

doctor should use such a phrase. "Yes, indeed, we really do need her, Traverse," said the doetcr's daughter. "Traverse I" It had made him proud to hear her call him, for the first time in his life, "Mr. Rocke," but it made him deeply happy to hear her call him "Traverse." It had such a sisterly sound coming from this sweet creature. How he wished that she really were his sister but then the idea of that fair, golden-haired, blue-eyed, white-robed angel being the sister of such a robust, rugged, sun-burned boy as himself! The thought was so absurd, extravagant, impossible, that the poor boy heaved an unconscious sigh. " Why, what's the matter, Traverse? What are you thinking of so intently ?" "Of your great goodness, sir, among other things." "Tut! let's hear no more of that. I please my- self," said the doctor • "and now, Traverse, let's go to work decently and in Order; but first let me settle this point: if your good little mother determines in our favour, Traverse, then of course you will live with us also, so I shall have my young medical assistant at hand. That will be very con- venient; and then we shall have no more long, lonesome evenings, Clara, shall we, dear? And now, Travene, I will mark out your course of study, and set you to work at once" "Shall leave the room, father 2" inquired Clara. "No, no, my dear; certainly not. I have not had you home so long as to get tired of the eight of you yet. No, Clare, no; you are not in our way—is she, Traverse ?" "Oh, sir, the idea—" stammered Traverse, blush- ing deeply to be so appealed to. In his way I why a pang had shot through his bosom at the very mention of her going. "Very well, then; here, Traverse—here are your books; you are to begin with this one; keep this Medical Dictionary at hand for reference. Bless me! it will bring back my own student days to go over the ground with you, my boy." Clara took her work-box and sat down to stitch a pair of dainty wristbands for her father's shirts. The doctor took up the morning papers. Traverse opened his book and commencd his readings. It was a quiet but by no means a dull circle. Occasionally Clara and her father ex- changed words, and once in a while the doctor looked over his pupil's shoulder, or gave him a direction. Traverse studied con amore and with intelligent appreciation. The presence of the doctor's lovely daughter, far from disturbing him, calmed and steadied his soul into a state of infinite content. If the presence of the beautiful girl was ever to become an agitating element, the hour had not yet come. So passed the time until the dinner-hell rang. By the express stipulation of the doctor himself, It was arranged that Traverse should always dine with his family. After dinner an hour, which the doctor called a digestive hour, was spent in loitering about, and then vie studies weee resumed. At six o'clock in the evening Traverse took leave of the doctor and his fair daughter and started for home. "Be sure to persuade your mother to come, Traverse," said Clara. "She will not need persuasion; she will be only too glad to come, Miss,' said Traverse, with a deep bow, turning and hurrying away towards home. With "winged feet" he ran down the wooded hill and got into the highway and hastenened on with such speed that in half an hour he reached his mother's little cottage. He was all agog with joy and eagerness to tell her the good news.

rHE RESIGNED SOUL. "This day be bread and peace my lot; All else beneath the sun Thou knowest if best bestowed or no'

And let thy will be done."

-POPE.

Poor Marah Rocke had schooled her soul to resignation; had taught herself just to do the duty of each day as it came, and leave the future— where indeed it must always remain—in the hands of God. Since the doctor's delicate and judicious kindness had cherished her life, some little health and cheerfulness had returned to her. Upon this particular evening of the day upon which Trav:Tse entered upon his medical studies, she felt ..,cry hopeful. The little cottage fire burned brightly; the hearth was swept clean; the tea-kettle was singing over the blaze; the tiny tea-table, with its two cups and sausars, and two plates and knives, was set; everything was neat, comfortable and cheerful for Traverse's return. Marah sat in her little low chair, putting the finishing touches to a set of flue shirts.

She was not anxiously looking for her son; for he had told her that he should stay at the doctor's until six o'clock; therefore she did not expect him until seven. But so fast had Traverse walked that just as the minute hand pointed to half-past six, the latch was raised and Traverse ran in—his face flushed with joy. The first thing he did was to run to his mother, fling his arms around her neck, and kiss her. Then be threw himself into his chair to take breath. "Now, then, what's the matter, Traverse You ook as if somebody had left you a fortune." "And so they have, or as good as done so I" ex- claimed Traverse, panting for breath. "What in the world do you mean?" exclaimed Marah, her thoughts naturally flying to Old Huai- cane, and suggested his possible repentance or re- lenting. "Read that, mother, read that 1" said Traverse, eagerly putting a note in her hand. She opened it, and read: WILLOW HEIGHTS—Monday.

DEAR MADAM :—My little daughter Clara, four- teen years of age, has just returned from boarding- school to pursue her studies at home. Among other things, she must learn domestic affairs, of which she knows nothing. If you will accept the position of housekeeper and matronly companion of my daughter, I will make the terms such as shall reconcile you to the change. We shall also do all that we can to make you happy. Traverse will ex- plain to you the details. Take time to think of it, but if possible let us have your answer by Traverse, when he comes to-morrow. If you accede to this proposition you will give my daughter and myself sincere satisfaction. Yours truly, WILLIAM DAY. Marah finished reading, and raised her eyes, full of amazement, to the face of her son. "Mother 1" said Traverse, speaking fast and eagerly, "they say they really cannot, do without you. They have troops of servants, but the old cook is in her dotage and does all sorts of strange things—such as frying buckwheat cakes in lamp- oil and the like." "Oh, hush I what exaggeration r "Well, I don't say she does that exactly, but she Isn't equal to her situation, without a housekeeper to look after her; and they want you very much indeed." "And what is to become of your home, if I break

up 7" suggested the mother. - "Oh, that is the very best of it! The doctor says if you consent to come, that I must also live there, and that then be can have his medical assistant always at hand, which will be very convenient." Marah smiled dubiously. "I do not understand it; but one thing I do know, Traverse: there is not such a man as the doctor appears in this world more than once in a hundred years." "Not in a thousand years, mother and as for his daughter—oh, you should see Miss Clara, mother! Her father calls her Clare—Olare Day— how the name suits her! She is so fair and bright 1 with such a warm, thoughtful, sunny smile that goes right to your heart 1 Her face is indeed like a clear day, and her beautiful smile is the sunshine that lights it up I" said the enthusiastic youth, whose admiration was as yet too simple and single- hearted and unselfish to tie his tongue. The mother smiled at his earnestness—smiled without the least misgiving; for to her apprehen- sion the youth was still a boy, to wonder at and admire beauty without being in the least danger of having his peace of mind disturbed by love. And as yet her idea of him was just. "And mother, of course you will go," said Traverse. "Oh, I do not know. The proposition was so sudden and unexpected, and is so serious and im- portant that I must take time to reflcct," said Mrs. Rocke, thoughtfully. "How much time, mother? Will until to-morrow morning do 7 It must, little mother, because I pro- mised to carry your consent back with me. Indeed I did, mother!" exclaimed the impatient boy. Mrs. Roche dropped her head upon her hand, as was her custom when in deep thought. Presently she said: "Travy, I'm afraid this is not a genuine offer of a situation of housekeeper. I'm afraid that it is only a ruse to cover a scheme of benevolence, and that they don't really want me, and I should only be in their way.'' "Now, mother, I do assure you, they do want you! Think of that young girl and elderly gentle- man—pan either of thcm take chargt of a large

establishment like that or Willow Heights?" "Well argued, Traverse; but granting that ther need a housekeeper, how do I know / would Ina them 7 " "Why you may take their own words for that. mother." "But how can they know 7 I am afraid the/ Would be disappointed. "Wait until they complain, mother." "I don't believe they ever would." "I don't believe they ever would have cause." 0 "Well, granting also that I should suit them---- The mother paused and sighed. Traverse filled up the blank by saying: "I suppose you mean if you should suit then:, they might not suit you." "No, Ido not mean that! lam sure they vrould suit me I but there is one in the world, who WAY one day come to reason and take bitter umbrage At the fact that / should accept a subordinate situation in any household," murmured Mrs. Rocke, almost unconsciously. "Then that 'one in the world,' whoever he, Ether or it may be, had better place you above the necessity. or else hold his, her, or its tongue l--- Mother, / think that goods thrown in our way b/ Providence had better be accepted, leaving the coli. sequences to Him!" "Traverse, dear, I shall pray over this matter to- night, and sleep on it; and He to whom even the fall of a sparrow is not indifferent will guide me: said Mrs. Rocke ; and here the debate ended. The remainder of the evening waa spent ie laudation of Clara Day, and in writing a letter to Herbert Greyson, at West Point, in which all these laudations were reiterated, and in the course of which Traverse wrote these innocent words—"l have known Clara Day scarcely twelve hours, and I admire her as much as I love you! and oh, Herbertl If you could only rise to be a major-general and marry Clara Day, I should Le the happiest fellow alive I" Would Traverse as willingly dispose of Clara's hand a year or two after this time 2 I trot not

The next morning after breakfast Mrs. Rocke gaVe I In her decision. "Tell the doctor, Traverse," she said, "that ii understand and appreciate his kindness ; that I will not break up my humble home as yet; but I will I lock up my house and come a month on trial; if can perform the duties of the situation sags' factorily, well and good I will remain; if not• why then, having my home still in possession, I can return to it." "Wise little mother she will not cut down the bridge behind her ! " exclaimed Traverse, joyfullY. as he bade his mother good-bye for the day, and hastened up to Willow Heights with her answer. This answer was received by the good doctor and his lovely daughter with delight as unfeigned as it was unselfish. They were pleased to have a good housekeeper; but they were far better pleased to offer a poor struggling mother a comfortable and even luxurious home. On the next Monday morning, Mrs. Rocke having completed all her arrangements, and closed up her house, entered upon the duties of her new situatioe- Clara gave her a large and airy bed-chamber for her own use, communicating with a smaller one for tlie, use of her son; besides this, as housekeeper, she h 0 of course the freedom of the whole house. Traverse watched with anxious vigilance to fica out whether the efforts of his mother really ler proved the condition of the housekeeping, and we! delighted to find that the coffee was clearer and finer flavoured; the bread whiter and lighter ; the cream richer, the butter fresher, and the beefsteak jucier than he had ever known them to be on the doctor's table: that on the dinner-table, from clixf to day, dishes succeeded each other in a We ordered variety and well-dressed style—in a word. that in every particulir, the comfort of the familf was greatly enhanced by the presence of the house' keeper, and that the doctor and his daughter knesf

it. While the doctor and the student were engagna In the library. Clara spent many hours of the moor leg in Mrs. Rocke's company learning the arts of domestic economy and considerably assiiting her in the preparation of delicate dishes. In the evening the doctor, Clara, Mrs. Rocke, n Traverse gathered around the fire as one family--; Mrs. Rocke and Clara engaged in needlework, an° the doctor or Traverse in reading aloud, Or their amusement, some agreeable book. Some' times Clara would richly entf;rtain them witn music—singing and accompanying herself upon the piano. An hour before bedtime the servants were ;11,7016 called in, and general family prayer offered up. Thus passed the quiet, pleasant, profitable dall' Traverse was fast falling into a delicious dreanit from which, as yet, no rude shock threatened W wake him. Willow Heights seemed to him Pariv dise, its inmates angels, and his own life—be tude I _ .

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Council was held at the Town Dall, on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough) presided, and there were also Present Aldermen Greenall, Sutton, Bolton, Fran- eomb,Hutchinson ; Councillors Shaw-Green,Garner, Eleaton, Baird, Shillcpck, Lee, Ellison, Tinnion, Duncan, Wilkinson, Cannell, Smethurst, Woods, Furness, Rogers, Davies, Longton, Roberts, Plump- ton, Skelton, Monks, Evans, Bennett, Hepherd, Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Madeley (librarian). THE WATCH COMMITTEE.

In addition to the minutes given in last week's " Examiner' ' this Committee recommended that an Ordinary pension for life be granted to Inspector Bowen, he having been incapaciated by infirmity for the per- formance of his duty ; and that lkenses for the perform- ance of stage plays be renewed for St. James' Schools and Christ Church parish-room. The MAYOR moved, and Alderman SUTTON seconded the confirmation of the minutes, which was agreed to. FINANCE AND ESTATE COMMITTEE.--A. LARGE TRANSACTION. The minutes of the a ove committee were ot a routine character The principal items referred to orders for payment of the half-year's interest and annuities due Ist January. 1896, amounting to Z 9,017 7s. n., and °niers for payment of £136,799 17s. 9i. for redemption of water annuities, and £4OO in redemption of bond on im- provement rate.

Councillor SEL!.w.GREESIiin moving the confirmation of the minutes, said it might be that the Council would ex- pect him to say, something with reference to the large amount of money that had been dealt with during the past month. Never had a more important financial matter been carried through so successfully by the Cor- poration, and the whole of it had been done without the payment of any commission or P rocurati3n fee to any financial agent or other. person. When the Corporation purchased the Water Works they agreed to do it by way aarlulPY, and to pay for each annuity 30 year's pur- uFase,the annuities on the ordinary shares being redeem- able in five years, and the annuities upon the preference P'ceek in 30 years. The five years expired on the Ist Inst. When the committee were considering how they could find the very large sum of money which would he required to pay the redemption money of these annul- they sought all the informition that could be 0b- ,.,12:_1ed from financial agents and others in London, and `lap from several eminent borough treasurers in other letta of the kingdom. They were advised to issue a They gave great consideration to this matter, they found there would be very considerable trouble ti dealing with the sinking fund, because unless the 11,,,ual proportion could be invested at the same rate Ehat they were paying to their stock holders loss :Letild be sustained ; and in addition to that they found Liere would be considerable labour and extra cost 2ected with the bookkeeping and management. •ftidea, it was only fair to say that their own Borough atteasurer was strongly opposed to issuing stock. They, jherefore, determined to try to obtain the money by the 'Mlle of bonds extending over periods not exceeding ten /ears- They deemed it prudent to offer to the holders 9f the annuities form little consideration, and they, the!efore, issued a circular asking such of them as _flotelred to allow the money to remain with the Corpora- etodo so at periods not exceeding ten years at 4.2 17s. 6d. per cent. Only £37,000 of the £lBO,OOO !required accepted the terms of the Corporation, and it, therefore, became necessary for the committee to obtain the remaining £140,000. They issued an advertisement ~,,"!ering £2 153. per cent., and he was happy to say that wiPlin a fortnight of the time when the money had to be Paid over to the annuitants every arrangement had been Made, and the money was under the control of the Cor- Duration. The annuities payable represented a sum ..,equal to £3 63. Bd. per cent, on the capitalized value of we annuities, and the money having been obtained on tl average of £2 15s. 10d. per cent., the saving of the 'A. 10d. per cent, would amount to a considerable sum. The water Committee would get the benefit, and it Would so benefit the revenue of the Water Committee that there was no need to fear in any degree a claim on th, rates to pay the sinking fund and interest and an- Unities still remaining payable on the investments. It Showed the excellent condition of the borough with !egard to its borrowing powers in the eyes of the invest- Public, and he was pleased to announce that a large Dre Portion of the sam they wanted had been obtained st £2 15s. per cent. ;Hear, hear.) The minutes of the Committee were then confirmed. THE HOSPITAL QUESTION. RIVERS COMMITTEE--THE SETTLEMENT WITH THE SHIP

CANAL --THAgKS TO COUNCILL')R ROBERTS,

Councillor ROBERTS moved the adoption of the minutes of this committee, which we published in our last issue. He said that the Council would see that the agreements which had so long been the subject of intercourse bet- ween the Corporation and the Manchester Ship Canal and the various parties concerned were practically brought to a friendly end. (Hear, hear.) He wished to take that opportunity of tendering his sincere thanks to the Rivers Association, Messrs. Fairclough and Co., and many other local traders for their assistance to him, and for contri- buting to bring about the solution of this very important arid difficult question. He thought they might congratu- late themselves upon having secured a settlement with- out litigation which was at once satisfactory fo themselves as a Corporation and to the traders of the town. (Hear, hear.) Councillor HEATON seconded the passing of the minutes and endorsed the words of the Chairman (Councillor Roberts) in his thanks to those outsiders who had ren- dered assistance. Councillor LONGTON asked if Councillor Roberts could give them further information, He would like to know if the agreements were to be printed,whether each mem- ber could not be supplied with a copy of them. Councillor MONKS said that he would like to say a few words relative to the services which the chairman of the committee had rendered in these negotiations. (Hear, hear.) None but members of the committee—and not even all of those—were actually aware of the great amount of time and attention which the matter had de- manied from Councillor Rolwrts—(hear, hear)—and the committee showed its sense in deputing the chairman and the Town Clerk to carry out the negotiations, and arrive at a satisfactory settlement. The committee had tried for twelve months to settle it,and at the end of the time they were as far off a solution of it as when they started, but since they had entrusted the Chairman and the Town Clerk with the matter a settlement lwas speedily come to. He thought it was only fair to the Chairman to state publicly his opinion of the capability he had shown in dealing with the subject,and to thank him for the efforts he had put forth to carry it out to a successful termination. (Hear. hear.) Alderman SurroN also said that he would like to add his testimony as to the way in which Councilor Roberts had dealt with the subject, and thanked him for the immense amount of time and devotion he had given to it.

Councillor ROBERTS replying te Councillor LonE,rton, remarked that there was nothing in the agreements which was not ready for ratification,'for it had all been submitted to and approved by the Council. If copies of the agreements were submitted to the members,it should be upon the understanding that no further discussion should take place upon them, but that all discussion was now closed. Every one had the full sanction of the Council and of all parties interested in it,and to his (the speaker's) mind it would be disastrous to re-open the matter. He accordingly asked them to allow the question to remain closed,and the decisions ready for the seals of the great authorities concerned. With regard to com- plimentary references to himself, he quite appreciated them but at the same time he felt that he was hardly de- serving of all the praise that had been bestowed upon him.He had been ably supported by his comtnittee,and he felt under a great burden of debt for the constant and invaluable services rendered to him by the Town Clerk on behalf of the town. (Hear.hear.) Both of them had felt, as natives of Warrington, that they had done the best for their fellow men, whose interests were bound up with their own. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Longton wished to speak again, but was ruled out of order.

FIRE ENGINE COMMITTEE.

Alderman HurcEuNsoN, in moving the confirmation of the mit .tes which were of a routine character, observed that 0, sorry to say that the Vigilance Committee appoin'.. • y the Council some months ago had not pre- sented their report. They had dine something, but at a very slow rate. Various improvsments were required to be made in the working of the Fire Brigade, and yet they had not yet received the report.

fhe MLYOR-: You are out of order. We understand all you mean, but the question is not on the minutes. kLaughter.) Councillor Smgraunsr seconded the motion.

Councillor WOODS did not know why it was called a "Vigilance" Committee. It was a committee of inquiry, firmed to see how the work of the Fire Engine Commit- tee could be improved.

Alderman Eltricamsow: I must rise to a point of order. This is not on the minutes, Mr. Mayor. (Hear, hear, and renewed laughter.) Councillor WOODS: The report was forwarded to the Fire Engine Committee some weeks ago. The MAYOR: You are out of order.

Councillor WOODS said the committee did not appear to be any nearer putting matters right than they were before the outbreak of the fire on Messrs. Skelton's premises. Councillor SMETHURST said the cornmitteel were quite prepared to discuss the question. Councillor BENNETT thought that such a long time had elapsed since the Vigilance Committee was appointed that the Council ought to be in possession of a report. The Towts OLERX remarked that he was to some extent responsible for the delay. The report had been in his hands for the last fortnight, but he had had so much work to do that it had been impossible to attend to it. He was also preparing the report of the experts, and the reports would be presented together- The minutes were then passed. WATER COMMITTEE. —THE PROPOSED SMALLPDX HOSP ITAL. A Iderman FICANCOMB moved the confirmation of the minutes of this Committee which appeared in our last week's issue, and said he hoped that the one with re- ference to Pear Tree Farm was not in any way binding to the Council, as he had heard many complaints about the proposed hospital from people living at Padgate. They were afraid that such a hospital would be detri- asgari that ent somewith mental to the health of the district. Councillor HEITHERD seconded. CouncillorSllA into GREEN d m ago thev entered the f7Rural months Di- strict Council to supply a certain portion of Orford with water. The terms were that 8 per cent. should be paid on the outlay, and it was expected that the inhabitants would have to pay more money per annum for some time to come. There was, however, already a revenue from the district more than equal to pay what they guaranteed to pay on the cost of extending the water main (Hear, hear.) Alderman SUTTON again alluded to the hospital ques- tion, and said it was doubtful whether they would ever find a site that would be suitable in every respect. He found that the Medical Officer was of opinion that the site proposed was not a suitable one, and he was afraid, therefore, that an effort would have to be made to pro- vide another one. Councillor ROBERTS said that for a considerable time, getting on for two years, the Health Committee had been trying to find a suitable site for a small-pox hospi- tal. Bearing in mind the fact that the district around Warrington was more or less populous,the Council cv-aild see how difficult it was to find a situation free from the objection so easily taken to an institution af that kind. If the Corporation would not come to their help he did not know what the committee were going to do. If it was found impossible to precure a site they would have, as a committee to throw the responsibility of unpre- paredness for a similar outbrt--ak to that which occurred a few years ago,on the shoulders of the Corper?.tion: The Health Committee could not be held responsible if they received no support from the Council as a whole. 113 was not prep:red to say that the site proposed was the best ; at any rate it was the best which offered itself at nreseac,and in the circumstances they had either to face the present state of absolute unpreparedness for a further outbreak of smallpox or make some arrangement as seemed to offer Itself in regard to the site under dis- cussion. (Hear, hear.) The interests of the town were above the interests of people who lived outside,and who had only themselves to consider. A great deal of op- position was fancied, but it was not an opposition that was likely to lead to any serious difficulty. (Hear, hear.) Alderman FRANCOMB said that as chairman of the Water Committee he did not make any opposition to the site on personal grounds, but when a number of people made complaints it was only fair that they should have a hearing. Since he proposed the adoption of the minutes of the committee, he had been informed by the Town Clerk that Dr. Gornall had expressed the opinion that the place proposed was thoroughly unsuitable for a Smallpox hospital. The minds of the people, therefore, could be at once set at rest. The minutes were then agreed to. GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE. The minutes of this committee which contained nothing of any note, were agreed to without comment, on the motion of the MAYOR seconded by Alderman SUTTON, and the Council rose.

VENEZUELA AND THE TRANS VAAL.

SOME PLAIN SPEAKING BY MR. JOHN BURNS,

Mr. John Burns, M.P., delivered his annual address to his coustituents last evening. Secure al ways of a welcome from the men of Battersea, his reception on this occasiou was extraordinarily warm. It was really his first important address since the last general electiorr. Then, seduced by arts which need not now be character- ised, some of "the baser sort" deserted him, and his majority was correspondirrgly diminished. It is admitted, however, on all sides that the enemy will never do so well again, and that the Liberal majority will never again touch that law-water mark. The title of the address last night was " The Old Year and the New." Of the old. Mr. Burns was not able to give a very satisfactory account, and still less was he able to speak well of the promise, so far, of the new. He feared we were now face to face with a crisis, or a series of crises, more complicated and possibly more pregnant with disaster than any since the time just before the Crimean war. He therefore proposed not to allow " conventionality to chloroform his conscience" nor to give way to the orthodox fear that members of Parlia- ment had in speaking on questions that required bold treatment and vigorous analysis. The situation,he felt sure, would be faced by the people of this country with calmness, courage, and real patriotism—characteristics that, to its eternal credit, had lately marked the conduct of the bulk of the English Press. Touching first on the American di4pute. he held that at the bottom there was a genuine feeling of brotherhood between the English and the American peoples.and that any estrange ment between them was the result of the operations of "gold hugs and railway kings." If we were right about Venezuela, fighting would not mend matters. If we were wrong, arbitration would not make them worse. As a working man and a member of Parliament. he protested against the notion of a resort to physical farce being even entertained—a sentiment received with immense applause by he meeting. It was for the situa- tion in Africa that Mr. Burns reserved his thunders. " I was for fifteen months," he said, " the servant of a chartered company, and I know how the little game is done." As to the South Africa Company, this was a concern, he declared, founded in fraud, buttressed by corruption, supported shamelessly by royal patronage, and backed by a subsidised reptile press. It had but- chered and dispossessed helpless tribes. The history of Bulowayo, Mashonaland, Matabeleland, Kululand, and the whole string of filibustering expeditions organised by Rhodes and his ruffians was perhaps more shameless than anything the East India Company did in India. The Jameson raid was another move in the game—another stage of the bolstering up when a financial collapse was about to take place. As corroboration of this, he referred by the way to such different authorities as the " Inves- tor's Review" and the organ of the Liberty and Property Defence League. The miners from Caiifornia,Coolgardie, Cornwall and Wales has been deluded.lf they did not have employment at the goldfields they must be employed as police or as swashbucklers. How, he asked, had Jame- son been able to collect 600 or 800 troopers at one parti- cular spot, and how was it that Sr J. Willoughby, the commander of the military forces of the Company, was where he ought to have been ten years ago—in gaol (Cheers.) how came it that officers of the British army came to be in the affray? The "appeal from Johannes- burg " was" a put up jab," organised by Cecil Rhodes. It was odd that this affair should have been precipitated now, instead of in April, when Parliament was sitting. Still, Mr. Chamberlain had repudiated Jameson boldly, handsomely, and well—(Cheers). To the surprise of everybody, Mr. Rhodes had repudiated Jameson also. " A mean dirty trick this, to go back on an old pal."— (Loud cheers.) An area sneak outside Pentonville would not have behaved so—(Renewed cheers.) Rhodes's ruin dated from the day he repudiated Jameson. He must be careful Pretoria did not prove his Panama. Coming to another matter he felt compelled to warn people about, Mr. Burns referred to the Queen, and offered a touching tribute to her as a sagacious sovereign and as a woman. Her Majesty had relations on many thrones. (Laughter.) He wished, by the way, she would keep her " ugly duckling" on the Continent in order. (Great cheering.) He believed the Queen desired sincerely the permanence of her house, her home, and the common country. He asked what was she allowing or ignoring at the present time ? She was allowing the Marquis of Lorne, her son-in law, to be a shareholder in one of the Char- tered syndicates. It was true he had only five shares, but that made the matter worse, because he did not appear to have faith in it. (Laughter.) But why did she allow the Duke of Fife to be a director of the Char- tered Company—(loud cheers)—and why did she allow and why did Parliament permit officers in the Diploma- tic, Consular,and other services to besmirch the national honour by using their love of gold and company-promoting to gull ignorant investors—people who without these tinsel attractions would have left Rhodes and Company to their fate. The Queen, who doubtless inspired the graceful message sent to America by her two sons ought to insist upon these men at once abandoning these schemes. (Cheers.) The Prince of Wales, too, should see that his patronage of the Imperial Institute did not bring Lim into disrepute and into contact with the rough side of the English character. (Cheers.) The situation (con- tinued Mr. Burns) was critical. Mr. Rhodes might deny his responsibiliy, but that was all humbug. Mr. Burns's " remedy" for the sisuation was that Lord Salisbury should at once revoke the charter of the South Africa Company, and that he should then send Lord Roberts with 15,000 British troops to sequestrate all the political, judicial, and administrative rights now enjoyed under the charter, alienating the lands the Company had secured by deceit and fraud, and estab- lishing the autonomy of the independent States and Republics. The Prime Minister should also dismiss all commissioners, high or low, who possessed shares in the Chartered Company and its auxiliary companies. He suggested also that a civil commission of ability and eminence should be despatched to the scene, and if he were asked to name any particular individual, he would name Mr. Asquith. Reverting to the German Em- peror's message, Mr. Burns said he was rather inclined to laugh at it. It was a poor return for be- ing Her Majesty's relation and for the cession of Heli- goland. (Loud cheers.) He thought the German Emperor should remember before he talked as he had talked that he had enemies at home to deal with. Con- cluding, Mr. Burns suggested that there were people in this country,as elsewhere,who had an interest in forcing on war, and asked the meeting to send a message to America and to Germany to the effect that. while Englishmen would fight to the death for national honour, they " would no longer be made the catspaw of commercial tapeworms, who would eat out the hard won earnings of the people."

THE WISE & THE OTHERWISE.

In one of his recent books Mr. Leslie Stephen gives it as his opinion that the knowledge attained by the wise can never be communicated to the multitude."

Ah, yes. But who are the" wise" and who belong to the" multitude ?" That's about as tough a question to settle as to say who are saints end who are sinners. Things are so mixed, you know. Can we ever be sure we are wise? Is it ever absolutely safe to call another man a fool? Whosoever can decide that is a wise man —and perhaps the only one in all the Queen's dominions.

Was our good friend Mr. Samuel Nicholls a wise man to permit himself to suffer pain for eleven years ? No, not perfectly so. Nobody suffers pain if he can help it. He couldn't help it, because he lacked just one bit of knowledge—how to stop it. fie knows now ; but, alack a-day, who shall restore time gone ? Wise people learn- from the experience of others. Possibly here's a lesson for you and me. We can do no more than skim the surface of Mr. Nicholl's story; the whole of it would make a book. In harvest time, 1880, he felt dull, sleepy, and fagged. Both body and mind were heavy and low like the atmos- phere before a thunderstorm. And for him the storm was coming. Presently pain took him in the knees' which swelled up so badly he could hardly stir. The pain in the muscles of his right arm and shoulder : not a mere grumbling ache, but pain so intense that he uses the adjective "frightful" in describing it.

Later on his hands became so swollen and drawn out of shape that he couldn't hold a fork or a spade (he works on a farm). "I was so stiff in the joints of my legs," he says, "that I used to stand up at the dinner table.

Take another expression, quoted literally from his own accounz : "On certain occasions the pain was so bad that I have hollered for eighteen hoary at a time, and have fainted as I sat before the fire." In Mercy's name, try to fancy that ! It makes one's heart sore with pity for him ; even now, when it's all over and gone. A martyr on the rack couldn't have suffered worse.

Mr. Nicholls says be got little or no sleep when he had these bad attacks, and often sat up or tried to walk about, instead of going to bed. He was never free from pain: and even when at his best—when the disease was giving him a kind of let-up or respite—he had great difficulty in getting about his work, and did but little at that.

We are not to suppose that our friend went through all this without an effort for relief. Quite the contrary. He consulted the doctors and applied hot poultices and other things in that line, such as embrocations, rubbing bottles, and so on. Momentary relief came of it, but nothing that looked in the least like a cure. Now remember that we have merely glanced at this case. The reader's imagination must picture its full his- tory. It covers years enough to make a man old—to make him resemble a ship that has stuck on the rocks and been hammered by the gales of half a score of win- ters. It is a wonder that he should have had anything left to build on, or any balance of courage or hope. Here's the conclusion in few words, his own words too: "In August, 1891," he says, "a little book hap- pened to fall into my hands, telling about a medicine known as Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, and I read in it of a case like mine being cured by this Syrup. I got a bottle from Mr. J. F. Cook, chemist, Holbeach, and after taking it a few days some of the stiffness and pain went out of my joints. I kept on, and not long after- wards I was as right as any man can be. I have had no ache or pain since, nearly,cighteen months. (Signed) Samuel Nicholls,The •GlebekFarm, Hougham, near Grant- ham, January 31st, 893."

Isn't it a comfort to know that such a case can be cured? "Yes, yes," say we, all of us. It was chronic inflammatory rheumatism. Now for the golden lesson it teaches. Rheumatism is a result and symptom of a torpid stomach and liver— indigestion and dyspepsia. The only way to cure rheuma- tism is to care indigestion and dyspepsia, the cause of it Mr. Nicholls knows this now.

And if we all bear this in mind, no doctor or philoso- pher can be any wiser than we in that important par- ticular. Add one thing more: Mother Seigel's. Cura- tive Syrup is the remedy.

DANGEROUS TRADES.

Professor Cognetti di Martiis, is showing that each occupation, mechanical or int,llectual, has its peculiar nervous disease, draws a harrowing picture of the dan- gers attending modern industrial life. He begins by discussing the perils to the nerves of open-air workers. Lightning is one of these. It kills twenty-two persons annually in England, and seventy-one in France, and leaves many of those who escape death with shattered nerves for life. The malarial fevers common to open-air workers, especially agricultural labourers, are in many cases followed by severe nervous disorders, and there is a true rural paralysis resulting ftom these fevers. Tetanus, the green germ of ,which thrives in swampy ground, and suostrokes, are both followed by distress- ing nervous disturbances. Str)ng light produces a nerv- ous affection to the eye, known as snow blindness, and one form of the disturbance, to which foundrymeia are very subject, makes a victim practically blind towards sunset,and after nightfall. Miners have painful nervous affections of the eye, but from opposite causes. Their troubles are in great part caused by the rarefaction of the oxygen in the air in which they work, and are ac- companied by strange illusions, such as the apparent swaying back and forth of objects in the fields of vision. Miners working in mountain shafts have the so-called mountain sickness, accompanied by headache, writhing of the body, hesitancy of movement, heart affeetiens, nausea and vomiting, sometimes followed by insensibility, delirium, and coma. Aeronauts have the same trouble. Men who work under high atmospheric pressure are even in a worse plight, The voice becomes metallic, utterance is difficult, and in the case of some,sounds impossible; hearing is impaired, muscles are knotted, smell and taste are lost, and the labourer handles his tool with difficulty. Seasickness is another nervous affection that has always defied the doctors, and no satisfactory remedy has ever been found for it. Too much school work causes epilepsy and St. Vitus' dance in children, besides impaired sight, head- ache, stammering, insomnia and fleeting hallucinations. Under the intense strain of modern commercial life, the business man becomes a victim to neurasthenia, and loses the power of mental application. The speed at which railway locomotives are driven on certain lines utterly breaks down the nerves of the drivers in a short time. The intense preoccupation and great manual exertion of the pianoforte player often produces paresis. Clarionet players have spasms of the tongue. Sewing machine makers, telegraphers, cigarmakers, button- makers and others who have to maintain a high speed at their work, are subject to nervous disturbances im- mediately affecting the part of the body especially under strain, but extending to other parts. Dentist's leg is paralytic affection of parts kept long undo pressure. Hammer paralysis comes to the man who has ORO arm constantly plying a tool of the striking kind. The pro- fessional bicyclist is subject to shocking nervous iiala- dies. He is likely to suffer especially from excessive weariness, loss of the power of attention, of critical sense, of judgment, and of all the high psychic manifes- tations. Persons accustomed to use the voice a great deal are subject to laryngeal spasms; watchmakers and others who use strong magnifying glasses, become near- sighted. In fact, every occupation exacts its penalty, phvsical or mental, and the shortening of the hours of labour seems to be the only remedy in sight.—" Science Siftings."

SHIP CANAL LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

Traffic upon the Ship Canal last week showed a con- siderable improvement on the preceding week's returns. Daring the seven days ended January 3, 136 sea-going cargo ships, with an aggregate tonnage of 38,515 .net registered tons, arrived at and sailed from the various docks, wharves,&c. which are comprised in the Customs port of Manchester, against 100 vessels of 31,454 tons during the former period. Coastwise traffic increased by 32 ships and 3,058 tons, and foreign traffic by four ships and 4,003 tons. Amongst the full cargoes imported were twenty of stone, seventeen of China clay, fifteen of general merchandise, six of timber and wood pulp,three of logwood, three of pig-iron, two of cotton, two of green fruit, and one each of pyrites, flints, bonc-ash,and resin. Amongst the exports were twenty general car- goes to Brazil, the Mediterranean,and other foreign and home ports ; whilst cargoes of coal were shipped to Bel- fast, the Isle of Man, and Copenhagen. Durinc, the week the steamers Manx Fairy, St. Mawes Castle, and Water Lily, which have since the opening of the canal been en- gaged in the pleasure traffic under the management of the Ship Canal Passenger Steamer Syndicate, left the docks for Liverpool to be sold.

THE SATURDAY EVENING CONCERT.

At last a sense of the value of the Saturday even- ing concerts seems to have taken hold of the people of Warrington, for on Saturaay night the Parr Hall, while not exactly crowded, was full both upstairs and below. Whatever may have been the reason for this sudden access of attendance the fact remains that none went away dissatisfie‘). Since the previous Saturday the stage had been enclosed by " scenery " and curtains, making a decided improvement in the appearance of the Hall. The principal attractions of the evening were Mr. Mel B. Spurr and Mademoiselle Marienne Tostia, the former a humorist and the latter a mandoliniste. Mr. Spurr came to Warrington with a good reputa- tion, and it will be generally accorded that he went away with a better. In style Mr. Spurr greatly resembles Grossmith, and while lacking the latter's finish, be is better adapted for success with the average concert audience. During the evening Mr. Spurr appeared four times and was encored on each occasion. His most succsssful and popular selections were " Snorkins," "Tin Gee Gee," " A Little Music," including " Her Golden Hair was Hanging Down Her Back,". al4 Mendelssohn, Chopin, Liszt, and Mozart, and, the "Xmas Pantomime." In the latter Mr. Spurr shows up the ridiculous side of pantomime in an admirable manner, and moreover presents it in a light which has never been thought of by the majority of theatre-gaers. Mdlle. Tostia created quite a furore with her exquisite playing on the mandoline, accompanied by Mdlle. Elsa Tostia on the piano. On this instrument Wine, Jostia is probably the best performer who has yet appeared in Warrington, for the amount of expression and force. which she extracts from it would previously have seemed incredible. For her first item " Rhap- sodie Hongroise," (Hauser) she was twice encored, while for the successive selections the audience could not show too much appreciation. There was only one lady vocalist, Miss Alice Williams, but she was a host in herself, and her interpretation of "Gitana " (Boscowitz) and "Whisper, and I shall hear," met with a very warm reception. "Cherry Ripe" was given as an encore to the latter song, and Miss Williams' voice further displayed its sweetness in the familiar air. Mr. Hargreaves Hudson was the baritone for the evening, but it cannot be said that his success was pronounced. An attempt at originality was apparently his aim. "The Lost Chord " he sang to an organ accompani- ment with the result that it was difficult to pro- perly connect the two. "Revenge " (Hatton) was a song he attempted with more vigour than expres- sion. An encore was accorded him, and he gave in response "The Yeoman's Wedding Song," and achieved a much greater success by it. Mr. Neville Hastings (George Cropper) is well known in War- rington, and some improvement was manifest in his style, though "Come into the garden Maud," proved more than he could render with the test effect. As a whole, the concert may be taken as one of the best of the miscellaneous class ever given in Warrington, and was as much encouraging to the audience as the dimensions of the latter were to the Corporation officials.

DROWNING FATALITY.

Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at the Police Station, on Tuesday, on the body of Jane Roberts, 46 Wellington-street, whose body was found in the river Mersey on Saturday afternoon. Charles Roberts, father of deceased, said that his daughter was a fustian cutter. She was 15 years of age. He last saw her alive on December 3rd. His wife was the last to see her alive. He did not suspect that she would take away her life, and there was no reason why she should do so.—By Inspector spinks: There was no one whom she visited near the " Ha'- penny" Bridge.—Mrs. Roberts said that she last saw her daughter alive on December 6th when she went to her work after dinner, There was nothing to cause her to take away Let life. She was reported missing to the police on Saturday December 7th.— John Ball, said that he knew deceased and saw her on December 6th, near seven o'clock at night. She was then near the " Ha'penny" Bridge. He did not speak to her. It was a very dark night., and he never saw her again alive.—Eva Stockton, 8, Twelve Houses, said that she saw the deceased on Friday night December 6th, at eight o'clock. She was then near Dixon's corner (one of the Twelve Houses). he asked her where she was going and she replied " nowhere." She then asked her to come with her, but she replied: " No, my legs brought me, and my legs will take me hack." Witness worked with the deceased at the cutting shop but she never complained of her work. She had left her work at seven o'clock that night.—P.C. Barlow,gave evidence to finding of the body. It was right in the weir, fast in the sluice. There were no marks of violence on the body. The mother was re-called and said that her husband was not harsh in any res- pect to the deceased. She was quite on friendiy terms with all the family. The jury returned a ver- dict of" Found drowned."

ONE CA.USE : MANY RESULTS.

It is very hard for some persons to understand that many diseases and ailments have one source. For ex- ample, Consumption and Scrofula are closely related, and are almost always the results of poor blood. The body does not get enough nourishment, the blood becomes depleted, vitality is low, and there is no resist- ance to disease.

I don't suppose that Mrs. Kirby knew the full signi- ficance of what she wrote when she sent the following letter, and she probably did not care for the science of it, for all she wanted was to give her children health,but at any rate my point is made clear by her letter. You can read it and judge for yours,!lf : "89, ADAMS ROAD, BRADFORD HOUSE, " FRATTON, PORTSMOUTH, Sept. f4tll, 1895. "Messrs. Scorr & BOWNE, Ltd., "47, Farrington Street, London, E.C.
