I have found that Scott's Emulsion has so many uses, and gives such splendid results, that I consider it an icvaluable remedy. •‘ I have three children, and as each one has had its share of illness, have certainly had my share of anxiety. " My daughter, at the age of nine years, shouted sign s of wasting. She had no appetite, and was thin and weak. A little book about Scott's Emulsion was left at our door, and after reading it, I thought I would try Scott's Emulsion for my daughter. My husband also thought this would be agoed idea,so he bought a bottle, and were very pleased to notice a decided improvement in her condition from this first bottle. We continued to give her the Emulsion, with the result that she gained n flesh and s trength and completely recovered her health.

" The next occasion that we had to use the Emulsion was when our baby-boy—ten months old—was in a les ak and emaciated condition. The total amount voluntarily contributed in the British Isles by all sections of Christians in the financial year 1894 for promoting missionary work was £1,375,571.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The monthly meeting of this Board was held in the Board room of the Workhouse, on Wednesday morning, Mr. William Houghton presiding. There were also present Mrs. Clay, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Rev. J. H. Riddette, Messrs. J. Houghton, A. J. Jolley, W. Hickman. T. B. Malkin, J. Clarke,A. Lang, G. Thompson, S. Davies, J. Warburton, J. Billinge, J. Mather, G. Pilling, J. W. Knowles, T. Halton, A. Jolley, J. Johnson, Dr. Patterson, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk), C. H. Ward (master), and H. M. Mines (superintendent).

AN ENTERTAINMENT TO THE INMATES.

Mrs. CLAY said that Miss Massey had offered to give a tea and entertainment to the inmates of the hospital on January 27th. On the motion of Mr. PILLING, seconded by Mr. A. JOLLEY, the offer was accepted.

PROCEEDINGS AGAINST THE RIXTON OVERSEERS.

The Clerk was authorisPd to take proceedings against the Rixton-with-Glazebrook overseers, who had not yet paid the last three calls.

ILLNESS OF THE REV. G. F. ROBERTS.

It was stated by Mrs. Clay and several other members that the Rev. G. F. Roberts was very ill, and general regret was expressed, the CHAIRMAN saying that Mr. Roberts was the most useful member that ever was on the Board.

===== 0002601_18960111_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
HOI7SX COMMITTKE.

This committee reported that £2OO be paid on account to Messrs. Bradford and Sons, the work being satisfac- tory. They also recommended that the tender of Mr. Robert Heaton for 40 tons of sleepers at 13s. 6d. per ton, be accep ed, and failing the acceptance of that tender, tkat of Mr.Denham at 14s.6d.per ton. The committee also reported that a deputation consisting of Messrs. Clarke, Thompson, A. Jolley, Pilling, Billinge, and the master had visited New Brighton for the purpose of seeing the drying stoves there, and they unanimously recommended that the tender of the arrangement suggested by Messrs. Summerscales of Keighley for £163 10s. be accepted.— Mr. Clarke in proposing the adoption of the minutes said that the matter of the heating arrangement had been ?ut off year after year, but they had now to face it. What had been done he thought that would be very satisfactory. Messrs.Summerscales' was considered to be the best scheme and the committee therefore recommended that such scheme should be adopted. Whereas the old stoves took 2i hours to dry, the new ones would not take one hour.—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY seconded the adoption of the minutes.—Mr. THOMPSON said that the new stoves would be heated by exhausted steam, and the steam which was now wasted would be capable of doing all the work.—The minutes were passed ununimously.

SCHOOLS COMMITTEE.

This Committee's minutes which recommended among other things, the purchase of a harmonium, to cost not more than £lO, for the use of the schools were also passed unanimously.

THE DINNERS TO THE INMATES.

The MASTER reported that the inmates of the House had their annual dinner of roast beef and plum pudding at Christmas, and it was very much enjoyed. The in- mates were very well-behaved. The Superintendent also gave a similar report with regard to the Padgate schools.

THE PROPOSED INFIRMARY.

The CLERIC read the minutes of the Infirmary Com- mittee, which, he said, did not involve any particular action. They stated that a letter bad been received from the Local Government Board suggesting that an endea- vour should be made to acquire the site of the Warring- ton Borough Infectious Diseases Hospital, if it could be obtained at a reasonable amount. The Clerk reported that he had communicated with the Town Clerk of Warrington on the subject, and the Corporation had de- cided that they would sell to the Guardians their hospi- tal for £lO,OOO. The Infirmary Committee of theßoard determined to make no recommendation for the present, and instructed him (Mr. Sutton) to write to the Local Government Board thereon. The subjects of loan, em- ployment of solicitor to prepare conveyance if land, notice to quit to the present tenantotc.,were considered, and without coming to any final decision upon the sub- jects there were severally left in the hands of the Clerk to ask as advisable upon the further decision ef the com- mittee.

The minutes were passed, on the motion of Mr. PIL- LING, seconded by Mr. ALFRED JOLLEY. The CLERK read the reply be proposed to write to thA Local Government Board explaining the present posi- tion of affairs, and it was approved 'by the Board.

A MOTION LtEIrERRZD.

A. motion of which Mr. Cross had given notice to the effect that repairs be put in charge of the Building and Repairs Committee was deferred until the next meet- ing, owing to ,the inability of that gentleman to be present.

A COMMUNICATION IROM A TEMPERANCE SOCIETY.

A communication to tile Chairman from the Society for the Prevention of the Sale of Intoxicating Liquors to Young Children was read to the Board, asking for his signature in favour of a bill to further the object of the society. The letter went on to picture the evil sights which were to be seen in public-houses, and hoped that the Chairman would lend his influence to keeping young children free from such temptation.—The CLERK said that the letter was simply to the chairman and not to the whole Board.—The CHAIRMAN said that the object seemed a worthy one, and he saw no objection to it. Be was however certain that they bad drawn a too highly coloured picture. Personally he should decline to have anything to do with it. If the Board wished thy could take it up.—Mr. KNOWLES said that as the letter was to the Chairman the matter was closed, and the con- versation then dropped.

TEE MEDICAL OFFICERSHIP OF NEWTON AND HAYDOCK.

The Rev. J. H. RinnErrE asked if some arrangement could not be made whereby the people of Haydock could be more convenienced than they were at present with regard to the medical officer. There was a number of people who had to walk two and a half miles before they could get medical relief. This matter wonld receive consideration at the next meeting of the local commit- tee, and he would like the Clerk to tell him whether there was an arrangement originally made that Dr. Watkins was required to have a surgery in flaydock.—The CLERK said that he had no recollection, and all the law required was that the medical officer should live within the dis- trict. Of course it was intended that he should be reasonably "getable," to all people. If the com- mittee would consider the question, and make such suggestions which they thought reasonable, those sug- gestions could be made to Dr. Watkins who could reply thereto. If there were any difficulty, then they could write to the Local Government Beard.—Mr. BiLurros said that he understood that Dr. Watkins had arrange- ments with doctors in Flaydock on the matter.—The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE said that he represented the West ward this was the furthest place from Dr. Watkins sur- gery. His constituents had therefore the furthest to walk. He thanked Mr. Sutton for his advice and the matter would be brought up at the next meeting of the local committee. CLASSIFICATION or CASUALS.

Mr. MA.Laix asked the Clerk whether he had received a circular from the %I, olverhampton Union asking for the support of the Warrinzton Union to a scheme for the distinguishing of bona fide workingmen from the profes- sional tramps. Be thought that the the workingmen who had to go about the country through no fault of their own, ought to have more humane treatment than was received at present.—The CLERK said that a circu- lar had been received ,but added that the Board had been represented on a deputation to the Local Government Board, and had their names on record in favour of such classifications. Of course the Board could pass a motion authorising him to write in favour of the Wolverhamp- ton Union proposal.—Mr. MALKIN moved, and Mr. CLARKE seconded that this be done.—After a few words form the Rev. J. H. Riddette and Mr. Hickman, the motion was passed, and the Board then arse.

110 W FOREIGN COMPETITION IS HELPED.

The British merchants at Yokohama are, says the "Daily News" correspondent, divided amongst them- selves in opiniAn upon Japanese competition with Eng- land. Thus while the Manchester people are complain- ing that Japan is now manufacturing cotton goods, it is remarkable that other English firms sell cotton-spin- ning machinery to the Japanese and provide thein with experts to instruct them in the processes of manufac- ture. Hitherto Japan has had to buy her ships abroad, mostly in England, but the " Hochishimbun " discloses an arrangement whereby, at no very distant date, Japan will not only do her own shipbuilding, but probably compete with England. The paper states that arrangements have been come to for the establishment, of a steel foundry by the Armstrong Company, of Eng- land, on the iollowing terms :-1. The material shall for the present be imported from England. 2. Of the workmen to be employed 20 per cent, shall be brought from England and 80 per cent, shall be Japanese. 3. When a new arm is invented in England it shall be manufactured at the works in Japan. 4. For a stated number of years the Japanese Goverument shall glve a fixed subsidy to the Company. 5. On the expiration of the period during which the Company receives a sub- sidy the works shall be sold to the Japanese Government.

The emigration returns for 1895 show an increase of about 45,000. Considerably over two-thirds ot the emi- grants were destined for the United States. For the Cape of Good Hope and Natal there were 26,018. against 16,760 in 1894.

THE LATE MR. J. S. GARLAND.

REFERENCE AT WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

Further testimony of the respect in which the late Mr. Garland was held, was afforded by the crowded appearance of Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening, when the Rev. John Yonge preached a special memorial sermon for the deceased gentle- man. The first part of the preacher's text was taken from one of the quotations on the funeral card and consisted of the words "1 will dwell in the House of the Lord for ever." Psalm 23, part of verse 6; the remainder was" He asked life of thee, and Thou gayest it him, even length of days for ever and ever," Psalm xxi. 4. After dealing with the mean- ing and value of the words of the text the rev, gentle- man expressed his sorrow at the loss Wycliffe had sus- tained in the death of such an untiring worker as Mr. Garland. Speaking of the private character of the late Mr. Gar'and, Mr. Yonge said he was not a man glib of speech upon the sacred matters of religious experience. He was characteristically quiet, and sank from any ob- trusive expressions of his inmost feeling. But if his inner conviction and feeling might he inferred from his customary habit of life, they might take it that he was seeking to live every day in the consciousness of God, and with a sincere desire to live aright. Like the best of men he doubtless had his incr ngruities and his dis- crepancies, his failings and infirnaities,but that his mind was ever bent towards God, and his desire earnest to- wards what was good and right, were amply evidenced by his prayerful habit, leading him to tke daily cultiva- tion and practice of private prayer, and to the regular observance in his home of that which was so rapidly be- coming crowded out of our far too busy and excited lives—he meant the observance of family worship. As a pastor, moreover, he could say that their friend's high- est commendations respecting the sermons and services at Wycliffe had been invariably in reference to those which had been most spiritual and devout in character.. Religion with him was by no means only a Sunday dross, or a church vestment. He hear ily believed in its con- trol of daily life, and he had no doubt that he gave evi- dence of that in many ways. He never spoke ill words of other people. However busy other tongues might be in spreading scandal, or in uttering harsh and unchari- table criticisms upon conduct, in assigning motives, or in passing judgments, his tongue never in- dulved in that pernicious bet very popular vice. Surely these were unmistakably t•igns of sincere disciple- ship, and of his sense of living every day in the House of God it became him as far as possible to live peaceably with all, and to do nothing to wound or grieve the sensibilities of any. The Bible and the hymn-hook were precious companions. He had ever loved the Holy Book, and had made it his careful study. When he was a teacher in the school, he never taught, as many did, without careful preparation. And so, having studied the Book, its truths beamed out more sweetly on him in his days of illness. A reference was made to a visit Mr. Yonge paid to Mr. Garland on Christmas Sun lay,when he asked him as to his state of mind. After expressing confidence of recovering and living for many years, he said that whatever happened all would be well. He began to re- peat very feebily " Just as I am." and he (the preacher) finished the verse for him. "That is not the verse I want," said he. "It is one of the later ones." Read- ing the hymn to him he came to the fifth verse : Just as I am, Thou wilt receive, Wilt welcome, parrion. cleanse,' relieve, Because Thy promise I believe. 0 Lamb of God, I come. " That is it," he said. " 'Because Thy promise I be- lieve ' " He was resting there. Was he not dwelling in the House of the Lord then ? Assuredly be was. He was dwelling in the secret place of the Most High. and abiding under the very shadow of the Almighty. There was a singular occurrence as he finished reading the verse, for just at the moment one of the Christmas bands struck up the very brmn,much to Mr. Garland's delight. The rev, gentleman made otherallusions to the character and life of Mr. Garland, all of which were listened to with deep attention and sympathy with the widow,son and relatives of the deceased who were present.

STOCKTON HEATH.

STOCKTON HEATH Sr. THOMAS'S Y.M. CLASS.—The Rev. T. S. Tofts (vicar), gave a very earnest New Year's ad- dress to the above class on Sunday afternoon, about 70 members being present, taking for his subject " A good soldier of Jesus Christ." It was attentively listened to. At the close, the vicar, on behalf of the teachers, and members, presented a bible to Mr. George Robin, who is leaving for Gibraltar, and also a hymn and prayer book as the Vicar's parting gift The words of advice to him will not be soon forgotten, either by him or the members present. In reply Mr. Robin said he should never forget the dear old class or his kind teacher, and he hoped one and all would value it more and more, for it had been a source of help to him. If he retu nod to the dear old village he hoped to have a place in it. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Robin, (Sen.) thanked all for their kindness to his son, especially to the vicar for his fatherly advice and after a few words from the teacher (Mr. Hallows), the gathering dispersed.,

the defeat of JAMESON was made known bet startled and angered the people of this country. It seemed to imply that in the opinion of the li.usEß President KRUGER might at any time call upon Germany to assist higi in raaaintaining the independence of the Transvaal. It was there- fore a clear disregard of a part of the Convention of 1884 which provides that the Boer Government shall enter into no relations with foreign powers except with the consent of the British Crown. The seriousness of this message from the German autocrat has not been lessened by further infor- mation. The E APEROR, it appears, not only sent a telegram but had resolved upon landing German marines at Delagoa Bay and sending them on the Pretoria, and had actuallimade an application to the Portuguese Government for authority to send these troops across Portuguese territory. Had the EMPEROR carried out this design it practically would have been an act of hostility against this country and there:can be no doubt that it would have been resented as such. The land- ing cf these marines was, fortunately, counter- manded, but the sudden display of German ill- fe3ling has not been lost either upon the people or the Government of this country. There is a very perceptible stiffening against all foreign demands from whatever quarter they may come, and the strengthening of the fleet is an unmistakeable in- timation that the Government thinks itadvi sable to be prepared for even the most serious em3r- gency.

LAST week we commented upon The Venezuela the apparent tendency to belittle Dispute. the importance of the points at issue between this country and the Unit:d Stat- -; during the present week tilere has been a decided change in that respect. It now seems to be recognised on every hand that this boundary dis- pute is not going to settle itself and that the sooner it is seriously faced the better it will be if it is to be settled by peaceful methods. Tnere .an be no doubt that feelings are being aroused on both sides of the Atlantic which tell distinctly against a peaceful result. Newspaper correspond- ents upon the other side report a recrudescence of the " jingo " spirit, and certainly no one can say that the events of the last few days have not created very decided symptoms of the "war fever" on this side of the Atlantic. So far as this coun- try is concerned the hot feelings that have been aroused have, as yet, been directed againit the interference of the impulsive Kaiser. But warlike passions are undiscriminating. A. casual incident or unhappy accident might at any time suddenly excite public animosities to such an extent that the task of peacemakers would be rendered hopeless. This is an almost inev it able result of the course taken by President CLEVELAND. The references he made to this country in his Message were decidedly unconciliatory and it may oven be said unfriendly. The Commission he has appointed is, occording to his own declaration;_to decide the question of peace or war. If we are to take the literal meaning of his words it is now not evea a question of arbitration. The Commission has been appointed to define the frontier of British Guiana and Great British is to be asked to accept this frontier or to enter upon a war with the United States. If Mr. CLEVELAND thought that such a Commission as this could carry out a prolonged inquiry without the relations between the two great communities that will be affected by its decision being in the meantime ruffled he must have an extremely meagre knowledge of huma n nature. As a matter of fact, although the Com- mission has not yet taken even a scrap of evidence the tension is already percep- tibly greater, and there are voices raised both here and in America warning us against allowing matters to drift any longer. There are several methods suggested by which the dispute might be ended. One is that England should recognise the Commission by appointing a similar Commission with a foreign Commissioner to give the casting vote. Another is that there should be a permanent Court of Arbitration estab- lished before which this Venezuela dispute and all other disputes between the two countries could be sent for settlement. There are other proposals for securing the same end but these appear to be the two most important. Whether any one of them will be accepted remains to be seen, but that there is imminent danger in a policy of " drift is uite evident.

THE EX A_MINER.

SATURDAY, January 11th, 1896.

SINCE the opening of the • year South African events of a sensational character TroublEs. have succeeded each other with startling rapidity in South Africa and elsewhere. The singularly rash and ill-advised freebooting raid. of Dr. JAMESON into the Transvaal, came, as everybody now knows, to a disastrous end. The information which has come to hand piecemeal during, the present week all goes to show that JAMESON and his followers were out-manoeuvred and out-numbered by the Boers. President KRUGER had evidently been much batter informed of the intended raid than was expected, as he had succeeded in establishing a well-armed force of two thousand men .between Johannesburg and the invading party. Under these circumstances the defeat of the Chartered Company's party was almost inevitable. When JAMESON reached the Boer position his horses were tired out and his men were without provisions. He nevertheless fought desperately for several hours in his endeavours to break through the op- posing lines, but in the end, after using up all his ammunition and losing heavily in killed and , wounded, his only choice lay between uncondi- tional surrender and the total annihilation of his force. The Boers,it must be admitted, have acted with prudence and magnanimity. The prisoners, who numbered between five and six hundrod,were taken to Pretori, the capital of the Transvaal,but were subjected to no indignity or outracte, while the wounded received every possible attention near the scene of the battle. Later on, it was announced that, as a re3ult of ne- gotiations carried on by Sir. HEneuLEs ROBINSON, the chief British representative in South Africa, the Boers had decided to hand over the whole of the prisoners, including Dr. JAMESON, to the British Government to be dealt with by them in accordance with the nature of their offence. This act of generous policy on the part of the Transvaal authorities will, it is needless to powerfuletffh Africa. Had he ectinclearing the troubled bill eadv eatamvoesrpyhe of son re lßeoweerrsedaesatlitlehyarmshigly w hteaitahilyJlAlamvEesodNeneand hisunde fol r the- sinpvuareoiefnthenifr fitrshetirrescennutnniternyt, atthetrleie cuainijushteitilaibullee doubt that the results so far as SJuth Africa was concerned would have been in the highest de- gree disastrous. . Probably not even the prompt- est and most decisive use of all the powers at the disposal of the imperial authorities at the Cape would have prevented the outbreak of a racial war through which both Dutch and British would have seriously suffered, but especially the Dutch. As it is the Boers have" heaped coals of fire" upon the heads of their enemies and detrac- tors. They have not only displayed bravery in the field but they have also skown humanity and wisdom in their subsequent policy. Sa far as they are cpncerned it might probably be said that the South African " crisis " is at an end, but un- fortunately much larger issues have ben raised .by the ill-starred invasion of the Transvaal. The 1 telegram of congratulation which the EMPEROR of GERENY despatched to Piesident KRUGER after

The town of Johannesburg which is the centre of so much interest at the present time is very appro- priately described as the "New Eldorado." It may also be described as the Chicago of South Africa. In 1886 the site on which it stands was not hing more nor less that bare veldt (like an American prairie) with perhaps a few Kaffir Kraals and a Boer's farm or two in sight." Now it is" a city of five broad streets and finer buildings—a city of elec- tric light and tramways, of hotels, clubs, restaur- ants, theatres, music-halls,cricket and polo grounds, and two race-courses—a city with a population of 611,000 souls, three daily and three weekly news- papers, only 49 hours from Cape Town and only 450 hours from London." It is said that in the city itself land fetches about as much per foot as it does in London, and fabulous sums are extracted from the gold mines iethe neighbourhood.

The Vienna correspondent of the London "Daily Chronicle" tells of some wonderful discoveries in photography which, it is claimed, have been made by a Professor Routgen of Wurzburg. This gentle- man we are told, can do more than "look you through and through—he can picture your very skeleton." Flesh and blood are no barriers to the lens of his camera. He can picture instead of probe for a bullet that has entered, and show on a gla"ss plate the "mischief it has done to the bony structure." It is, furthermore, stated that this Professor of Wurzburg can photograph metals when they are covered with wood, and, to cap all, he can do all these things when a cover is put over the lens. It is a wonderful story, but as a commenta- tor upon these reputed discoveries has said—" see- ing is believing."- Professor Routgeu must come forward and convince a sceptical generation.

Se TuE. n 1 1.4 Fort nit t is) e' Th annual C HmEe Chester Farmers'l3. Club was held at the Hop Pole Hotel Warrington on Saturday afternoon, Mr. John Cooke (Tattenliltll), presiding. Mr. C. W. Dutton (hon. secretary) reported that, the accounts of the club showed an increase of about £3O on the year, but they had to provide for a special contingency, which would practically exhaust the surplus. On the proposition of Mr. T. J. Dutton; seconded by Mr. Mountfield (Kelsall), Mr. Walter Harrison (New Pole, Frods- ham), one of the retiring vice-presidents, was unanimously elected president in succession to Mr. Cooke. For the two vice-presidentships Messrs. William Parker (Pigeon House, Handley) and W. W. Bower (Nianor Farm, Hawarden) were unani- mously elected, but the, latter declined to serve. Most of the members of the committee were re-elected, with the following additioio :—Messrs. William Parker, Stamney ; Joseph Hall, Kingswood,. near Frodsham ; Robert Wade, Acton Grange, near Warrington ; and George Williams, Saltney. Mr. Charles W. Dutton was unanimously re-sppointed hon. secretary and treasurer; and votes of thanks to the retiring president and secretary were unani- mously accorded. Messrs. J. Roberts and NV. Be was standing by one of the stumps I at a lock when he was crushed between it and the arm of a lock gate. Amongst the changes which are notified in the Cheshire constabulary, is the removal from Runcorn of Constable Richardson to Norttiwich. His place will be filled by P.C. Carrington, who was formerly at Runcorn.

PRESENTATION TO AN ODDFELLOW.

P. G. Roland Jones, a member of the Loyal William IV. loige of Olifellows' Runcorn,has been the recipient of a splendid certificate from his brethren. The treasurer of the lodge P.P.G.M. Wright made the presentation,and referred to the services of Bro. Jones to the lodge. THE SHIPMENTS OF SALT FROM RUNCORN AND WESTON POINT.

There was a marked decline in the amount of salt shipped from Runcorn and Weston Point during last month. The number of tons shipped from Runcorn, both of white and rocked salts totalled 960 against 5727 in the corresponding month of 1894. From Weston Point there were shipped 801 tons against 2176 in December of the previous year. It will thus be seen that there has been a decrease of over 6,000 tons despatched from this district.

MISSIONARY WORK IN INDIA.

On Thursday afternoon, an intezesting address on missionary work in India was delivered in the Seamen's Institute, Runcorn, by Miss Eaton, principal of the high school for English girls at Simla, in the north west province of India, and formerly the head-mistress of the Wallasey and Preston high schools for girls. There was a good attendance. The proceedings were presided over by the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood. Miss Eaton stated that although she was .not directly associated with missionary work she had taken a deep interest in what was being done. She alluded to the Ze,nana work as well as that carried on by males in the schools in the way of religious education. She described the internal working of the Zenana, and stated that there would be the greatest hope for India when England could sent out Christian medical men and women. She also thought the support of orphans who had been practically aban- doned by their parents and who were now being brought up by the missionaries in schools was likely to be a very fruitful source of hope in future because of the influence they would use. Although the Government was doing its best to prevent infanticide it prevailed to a great extent especially as concerned females. Miss Eaton's services were acknowledged on the motion of the Rev. Canon Wood.

MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN,

RUNCORN.

The annual tea meeting in connection with the Runcorn branch of the above-mentioned mission, held on Monday evening in the Seamen's Institute, was the most successful which has been held under these auspices. The attendance at tea was so large as to necessitate a second sitting. The tables were presided over by Mesdames Abel, Whittaker, De Korte' Horsefield, Digges, Shaw, Cook,Mack, Water- worth, Evans, R. Shaw, Lambert (Liverpool), W. Shaw, Misses Hayes ;Frodsham), Heasley. Randles, Owen, Bowyer, and Rosenberg. Whilst the tables were being removed for the after proceedings an adjournment was made to the church, where hymns were sung. In the absence of the Rev. Canon Wood, the Rev. E. Lambert (chaplain of the Mission) presided at the meet- ing, and he was shpported on the platform by the R cv. Alan Williams (chaplain on the training ship Indefatig- able). Mrs. Lambert, Mr. Shaw (missionary of the Run- corn branch), and Miss Hayes (Frodsham), The Chair- man in the course of a few introductory remark& wished all present" A Happy New Year," Referring to the large company present he said the Vicar would have been delighted to have seen such a number of sailors and watermen present. It showed that good work was being done by Mr. Shaw, their Runcorn missionary. That gentleman had a marvellous way of getting so many voluntary helpers to assist him in that greed work. Their first business that evening was to make a presentation to Mr. and Mrs. Allen, now of Earlestown, but who had been labouring in the cause of the Runcorn branch for upwards of fifteen years. He then called upon Mr. William Shaw, one of the workers in the Sunday school, to make the presentation.—ln doing so, Mr. Shaw referred to the great services of both Mr. and Mrs. Allen in connection with their Runcorn branch. The address which had been most artistically prepared state I that the fellow workers of the mission desired on the termination of Mr. and Mrs. Alien's con- nection with them to present that address as an expres- sion of the esteem in which they held them and in recog- nition of their devoted service to their noble cause for so mauy years. The address was signed by Mr. Shaw ;missionary), Mr. William Shaw (teacher of the young men's select class), Mn. Mack (teacher of the first class girls), aril David Price (secretary).—Mr. Allen, in responding, apologised for the absence of his wife who was absent though indisposition, and expressed his gratitude to those who had contributed to the presenta- tion.

The musical portion of the programme was then pro- ceeded with. Songs were given by Miss Fleatherington, Miss Ada Randslls, Mr. Elli4nn,Mr. Woods,Mr. S. Cross; a duet was contributed by Mr. J. Moores and son; and Miss Annie Rogers recited. During an interval the Rev. Alan Williams mada a few appropriate remarks, and proposed a vote of thanks to those who had assisted in providing the tea, and also the woollen goods which were to be presented to the sailors.—Mr. Shaw seconded the vote of thanks, and re- marked that the tea meeting was the most successful which had been held since he had been connected with the mission. He hoped the sailors wfmli make good use of the mufflers and other goods which were to be dis- tributed. _

The Chairman then called upon Miss Hayes to distri- bute the articles. Three cheers were lustily given for the chaplain, and also for Mt . and Mrs. Allen. On Tuesday evening the scholars attending the Sunday school had their annual treat when upwards of 200 sat down to a substantial tea. Their wants were attended to by Mrs. Mack, Mrs. Cook, Mrs. Evans, Mrs. Hurst, Mrs. W. Shaw, jun., Miss Bowyer,Miss Waterwortb,and Miss Owen. After tea a Christmas tree, containing a quantity of useful articles, was stripped and each child received some gift. Mr. Hailwood distributed them. At the close each child received an orange, the gift of Mr. Cook.

THE NEW REGISTEftS FOR NORTH WICH DIVISION.

The new registers for the Northwich division and the township of Runcorn, which in common with those of the whole of the county have just been published by the County Council,are of considerable local interest as bear- ing on the rate of progress in the Parliamentary division or the town of Runcorn. Dealing with the Parliamen- tary register, the new one contains the names of 12,085 electors as against 11,788 for the year 1895, being a net Increase of 297. The following polling districts show a decrease: Davenhani. 3; Halton, 5; Runcorn North, 49 ; and Weaverham. 5. The remainder contain an in- crease : Barnton, 64: Marston, 7 ; Middlewich, 27 ; Northwich (Castle),. 35 ; Northwich (Leftwich), 32 ; Northwich (Witon), 63 ; Runcorn South. 53 ; Weston, 5; Winningtou. 56 ; Winsford (Over). 16 ; and Winsford (Wharton) 3. Aston,which is the smallest polling district in the division and purely agricultural, I:as exactly the same number on the register this year as last. The parochial register for the township cf Runcorn rontains an electorate of 3,635 distributed amongst the six wards into which .Runcorn is divided as follows : Halton Ward Mersey Central „ Bridgewater,, Newtown „ Heath

U

3,635

There are 296 occupation mad ward duplicate :registra- tions so that the net electorate is 3,339. A ceniparison with last year shows that the northern portion—the part lying below the Big Pool and the Bridgewater Canal—has a smaller electorate, while the urper, or southern, part has but correspondingly increased, thus proving what has been too painfully evident that Runcorn, commer- cially. is at a standstill. It it, to he hoped that the long looked for increase in the trades of the district may soon I be an accomplished fact, and continuous and satisfactory I progress again the lot of Runcorn and its surrounding district.

Pk PUBLIM'S LTCENSE ENDORSED AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday morning before Mr. James Handley (chairman), and Mr. F. J. Norman, Joseph Smith was summoned for being drunk on his own licensed premises, the Duke of Wellington, Percival-lane, at 12.45 on the 24th December,and further charged with permitting drunkenness at the same time and place ; Hanuah Smith, his wife, was charged with being drunk on the premises ; also Annie Shaw and James Hind, at the same time and place.—Mr. Caldercott, solicitor, Chester, prosecuted, and Mr. A. Browne, solicitor,'War- rington, defended. In opening the case, Mr. Caldercott said the facts were shortly these, and he thought they were very clear indeed in this case. On the day in question, P.C. Gre- gory was returning along the railway siding from Wes- ton to Runcorn. The siding ran near the rear of the Duke of Wellington, and as he was passing he saw Joseph Smith fall down in his yard and commence to vomit. He came to Runcorn and reported what he had seen to Sergeant Lee. They then went, to the house, and found Smith in such a drunken state on the sofa that he could not more. His wife was also drunk and tumbled down in the taproom, -and one of the officers , had to assist her on her legs. The other two defendants were also thero,and were in such a state that not one of them could say a rational word. The woman Shaw told the licensee and his wife that if they, were drunk it did not matter as it was in their own kitchen. The servant said she would take charge of the business, and the ser- geant instrudted her not to supply any of those in the kitchen with any more drink. Later in the day Inspec- tor Hicks went with Sergeant Lee and found almost the same state of things. Smith was then in such a drunken state that he did not know what he was doing. The tap-room floor was covered with wet, and the attention of the servant was called to it and she admitted that it was in a very dirty state indeed. Mr. Browne said that before the case was further pro- ceeded with, he wished to raise the point whether ad- mitting that Mrs. Smith was drunk the licensee could be proceeded against for permitting drunkenness. The Clerk said he thought the same law would apply as in the case of anyone else. P.C. Gregory then gave evidence in support of the above statemcnt.—Mr. Browne (in cross-examination) : Do you know that Mr. Smith has a drunken wife ? I know they are both drunken.—Do you know that when she gets drunk she is sent off to bed at once? Ye-,and they do the same with her husband.—Mr. Browne re- monstrated with the witness for not answering the ques- tions properly, and stated that he was not going to say that Mrs. Smith was sober; he could not.. . .

- Sergeant Lee corroborated the previous witness's statement regarding his first visit to the house. Inspector Hicics also gave evinence as to what he saw on his visit when accompanied by Sergeant Lee, and bore out that officer's statement. The taproom was in one of the filthiest conditions he had ever seen in his life.

.._ _ For the defence, Mr. Browne said he had made a sug- gestion, but it appeared that the superintendent was not allowed any discretion. It would have materially shortened the case. He was not going to say that the evidece regarding Mrs. Smith was untrue after the evidence of P.C. Gregory and the other two officers. With regard to that fact he would like to say that Mr. Smith had unfortunately a drunken wife, and that she had carried on this drinking business until he was sickened and tired of it. Those proceedings had re- sulted in consequence of her condition and he was not going to deny it. He had suggested that the case should be allowed to stand there, and the decision left with the magistrates as to whether they considered it was permit- ting drunkenness. With regard to Annie Shaw she had not been in the house more than two or three minutes when the officers came. She had come for eggs which Smith's sold, and not for drink. It was quite true that she said " Never mind you are in your own kitchen," to Mr. Smith, but those were not the words of a drunken woman. He suggested that it was a private kitchen,and that the licensee had a perfect right to invite anyone into it. With regard to Hind he came into the house by the back kitchen to see about a tontine club which was in connection with the house, and the police never spake to him. He had had no drink in the kitchen, and only ore pint earlier in the day. He would ask his friend to accept his suggestion.—Mr. Caldercott said he could not see that a int.n had any right to have his wife drunk on the premises any more than any other person. It was not likely that he was going to withdraw the case and probably be defeated on a point of faw.—Proceeding Mr. Browne said if the magistrates did find that the licensee was drunk. he wished to point out that Smith had been teetotal for seven weeks previously. He had had some friends visiting him but he only took four glasses of beer. He coold not be very drunk and was capab!e of transacting his business and conducted it until eleven o'clock at night. It was Christmas time and he thought some allowance should be made even to publicans. James Hind, porter at the locks, 11, Shaw-st., stated that he was perfectly sober on the day in question. s Alice Molyneux. servant at the Duke of Wellington, stated that Annie Shaw came to the house to see about some new laid eggs. She was not served with drink. Mr. Smith had had some drink but he was not drunk, and transacted his business. He had been sober for seven weeks. In answer to the chairman witness said she was in charge of the bar from seven o'clock in the morning. Qurstioned as to Mrs: Smith being drunk she said that " She was Pretty well on." Hind was crate sober. William Bolton, shopkeeper, Percival-lane, said he visited the Duke of Wellington between 1.30 and 2 o'clock. He had a conversation with Mr. Smith who seemed sober. Walter Anderton said Smith had had a drop of drink but was capable of conducting the business. , Annie Shaw denied that she-was drunk, and contra- dicted the statement that she was at the house later in the afternoon. It was true that she spoke to the officers, but she did not think she was doing anything wrong. James Robinson and George Gilmour spoke to visiting . ._. . .

the house at 5.30 on the night in question. Smith was-, they said, then capable of taking care of his business. The magistrates retired to consider their decision, and after a short deliberation the Chairman said they had given careful consideration to the case, and found that the charges were proved. Joseph Smith would be fined 10s. and costs for being drunk on his licensed premises; Mrs. Smith, his wife, 10s. and Costs; Annie Shaw and James Hind ss. and costs; and with regard to the more serious charge of permitting drunkenness. Smith would be fined 403. and costs and the license would be endorsed. Mr. Caldercott applied for the advocate's fee to be paid by the defendant, and was granted it. A second case against Mr. and Mrs. Smith charging them with being drunk on the licensed premises on the 28th ult. was withdrawn on payment of costs.

THE COUNTY PRECEPT.

The Clerk to the Cheshire Clunty Council (Mr. R. Potts) forwarded a precept upon the Guardians before the 27th January for the sum of £3;680 18s. 101 par- ticulars of which have previously been given.—The Clerk said it compared very favourably with previous ones. It was a reduction of about £3OO on the corresponding half year, and £5OO less than the midsummer call. TREAT TO THE CHILDREN.

_ The Chairman of the Visiting Committee (Mr. Stokes) thanked the members for subscribing., the sum of £193. for sweetmeats for the children of the house.

FINANCE!. ,

Mr. Heywood submitted itW re-port of the Finance Committee, reeommending the payments of accounts amounting to £1,697. The out-relief was as follows : Runcorn £95. Frodsham 30, and Budworth £2O. Mr. HeYwood mentioned that one account was for the sum of £2 195. for school fees. They had frequently recom- mended that the Runcorn Guardians should endeavour to get the schools free. There was one school in particular where they were charging fees,and it was aboard school in one ef the poorest parts of the town. He referred to Granville-street school. They had decided 't pay the ac- counts on that occasion,but he hoped the Runcorn School would not charge the Guardians for fees in future. —Mr. Taylor said so far as the School Board was con- cerned he did not think ths Guardians would be treubled with those accounts again,but as regarded the °the other denominational schools he could not say.—Mr. T- Loa stated that the Guardians w.auld not have any more ac- counts from Granville-street schools.—Mr. Heywood hoped they would use their influence to get the other schools free in Runcorn. It was the only place in the Union where the (Tnardians paid fees. Re further inti- mated that every call thronhout the Union had been paid. The balance in hand was £3243. The report was adopted.

THE RE-ARRANGEMENT OF DUTIES. In accordance with notice given, Mr. stokes moved that lunch be provided at 11.30 instead of noon : that the visiting committee meet at 12 noon ; that the Guar- dians meet at 12.45 p.m. ; that the relief cases be taken in the Board room by the Board resolving itself at each meeting into three committies and that new or impor- tant relief cases be submitted to tho whole Board.—Mr. Mclntyre suggested that the whole question should be deferred to the annual meeting of the Board in April. Mr. Stokes aareed to withdraw his motion and Mr. Mclntyre's suggestion was aereed to. This was all the business of public interest.

HALTON.

THE " WESLEY " GUILD.—On Monday evening, Mr. Allan Bag,uley, of Bangor College, gave a very intereo.- ing address on "How plants grow." There were 29 members and five companions present, also a goodly number of friends. The Rev. C. de P. Gliddon presided. At the close of the meeting one person joined the guild as a member, and two as companions.

"LITTLE SHORT OF A MIRACLE."

SURPRISING AFFAIR IN YORKSHIRE. Ejaculations of surprise greeted Miss Elizabeth Ems • ley,when met in the streets by former companions. The The latter could scarcely believe their eyes. Miss Ems- ley had been seriously ill ; the doctors appeared to do her no good, and her cure was considered little short of a miracle. The case was noised abroad, and to make an independent inquiry, a reporter of the " Pudsey News" recently visited Mr. Emsley's home at N 0.9, Valley-road, Littlemore. He says : "I was accorded a hearty wel- come. Mrs. EmAey told the story of her daughter's case. She said : • Our Lizzie commenced to grow ill a year last Whitsuntide, that is,at Whitsuntide, 1894. She was then sixteen peals old. We dallied a fortnight, but the pains all Over her booy, particularly in her legs and hands, became so bad that we were obliged to call in a most able doctor, who attended her for three months, but without recovery of health. The doctor declared it to be a case of rheumatism. She was not able to walk (without assistance) or kneel, and hcr bands were so much swollen that she could not have wrung a cloth if it had been to save tier life.'"

AN INTERRUPTION.

At this juncture of the interview a noise was heard outside, and one of the little ones cried "Our Lizzie's coming, mother," and immediately the subject of the interview came smilingly forward, and joined in the conversation. She had heard her mother's last remarks, and exclaimed, " Yes, sir, I could not stir ; my father had to carry me to bed at nights, and fetch me down again in the morning. and I had to be' paddled' about just like a child." ",Yes, that is so," put in the mother, "and she

===== 0002601_18960111_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
COULD NEITHER DRESS NOR UNDRESS

herself and besides being unable to walk or use her hands, she could not even feed herself. Day after day and week after week passed without any signs of im- provement, and she was c,oufined to her bed. "Well," continued Mrs. Emsley, we did not know what to do for her. We would take hold of her one on each side, and guide her to a neighbour's house fronting on to the main road,where she could sit in the doorway, and receive some pleasure by seeing people pass. Every- thing it was possible to get,which people recommended, I think we got, but of no avail." "But she does not seem at all ailing now! What has cured her ?" queried the reporter.

" Well," replied the mother, " we had read so much about the marvellous cures by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People that at last I said to my husband : What do you think if we try these Pills ; to which he re- sponded Well we have tried everything. I think we can try them.' Accordingly I went for a box. After taking the first box she could walk better. We continued get- ting the Pills,and she has mended ever since, and now sir, she can do anything as well as anybody almost. Ise, they have done her £2O worth of good, and she is new taking the twelfth box." "Do you honehtly believe aad attribute your daugh- ter's recovery to these Pills?" asked the interviewer, to which Mrs. Emsley emphatically answered " I do sir, awl to nothing else," an opinion in which the father and the girl herself were equally confident. " And " con- tinued Mrs. Em3ley, " she has never had anything else since she started taking them. Before, she had no ap- petite, but now she

CAN GET VP AT ANY TIME, Is always ready for her meals, and can eat first-rate too."

"Have you any objection to this interview being pub- lished ?" asked the reporter,to which both the father and mother promptly replied "No, not the slightest. It is true, as scores of people who are familiar with the case can testify, and who have told us that it should be made known, and we think that it is only right that people who are similarly suffering should know of it."

Mrs. E nsley mentioned several persons in the vicinity who had derived advantage front taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills on hearing of her daughter's case and cure. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills act directly on the blood, and thus it is that they are so famous for the cure of rheu matism, influenza, antemia, scrofula, chronic erysipelas, and to restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health. They are also a splendid nerve and spinal tonic, and thus have ctered many cases of paralysis, locomotor ataxy, neuralgia, St. Vitus' dance, and nervous headache. They are now obtainable of all chemists, and from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn Viaduct, London, at 2s. 9d. a box, or six for 13s. 9d., 'Alt are gebuine only with full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. A specific for all ladies' troubles, and for the numerous ailments of men which result from worry, and excesses, whether of work or otherwise.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION. WARRINGTON v. WIDNES.

The first meeting of these old rivals was rendered additionally interesting by the fact that the Warrington people had their firbt opr.ortunity of witnessing a game under the new rules of the Northern Union. There was consequently a very large crowd of about 8,000 on the Wilderspool Causeway ground at the time of the kick off. Most of the games which have taken place in pre- vious years between Warrington and Widnes have been very close affairs, and that on Saturday was no excep- tion to the rule, for it was fought with great stubborn- ness though with great good temper. The weather was just the sort for football, not being too cold, nor yet uncomfortably hot, and therefore the game was ex- tremely open and fast. The pace told very much on some of the players before the game was concluded. Mr. Court,of Oldham, was the referee, and the following were the Warrington players : Heesom, full back ; Barber, Burton, Cromand Broady, threequarters ; Clarke and Houghton, half backs ; Burrows, Nevins Fair- hurst, Hill, Barrett, Andrews, Thornton, and Swift. for- wards.

Barber, of Widnes, kicked off and Clark sent in touch. Houghton from the ensuing scrimmage kicked almost to centre. and Barber (Widnes) being penalised under the new rule, Burton took the free kick and gained on Brennan's return. A smart dribble by Burrows, Fair- hurst, and Broady, ended in the latter passing all op- position, and just as he appeared to have the line at his mercy, he missed ca,tchimr, the ball. Houghton passed out but Cross was floored, and Drummond getting pos- session, passed to Jones who kicked off and followed up having a clear field, but Broady after an exciting race just kicked dead in time. Widnes still attacked, and Drummond had a drop at goal whioh just went under the posts and another minor was given. Mid field play followed Cross's drop out,but good pasting between Burton, Clark. Broady, and Nevins enabled the latter to put his side on the offen- sive. A good run and kick by Barber (Warrington) was replied by Peters and Rispan and the latter was tackled just in time. A free kick given to Barber just fell out- side the goalposts and a scrimmage under the posts was got away by Clark. Fine passing taken part in by Clark, Houghton, Barber, Burton. and Broady enabled the latter to get to half-way,and Thornton almost scored immediately afterwards, but kicking too hard, the ball went dead. Clark got over but was called back owing to a Widnes man having knocked en the ball previous to his getting possession of it. Nevitt relieved the danger, and play again settled in midfield. Burton kicked well in the midst of his opponents-, and Clark taking a free kick put Warrington further on the offensive. HaN3OM rather surprised his opponents by a dashing run through the Widnes team and kick in touch. Irairhurst charged a kick of Drummond's and followed up so well that a line out took place on the line. Things looked danger- ous for Widnes until Barber kicked out and centre was reached. Barber of Warrington got hold now, and kick- ing over Brennan's head was glaringly obstructed,othor- wise a score would certainly have been made, but Mr. Court would not listen to the Warrington appeals. Peters Nevitt.and Rispan by a fine bout of passing took play to the other end when Broady by a splendid run and pass to Barber Rot to the half way line, but the remaining portion of the half was in favour of WiJnes, and the in- terval arrived with a blank sheet.

A sensational start was made after the interval, for after the usual exchanges, Houghton got possession of the ball near the half way line, and sprinted to the line, actually getting over,but was held up by his opponents. He parted to Barber who was in near attendance, and the home skipper was also held up, thus Widnes had the narrowest of narrow escapes in preventing a score. A prolonged attack was now made on the Widnes stronghold, and the defenders were given plenty of work to do, which they accomplished in a very fine manner, preventing all attempts no matter how well meant of the Warrintton players to get over the coveted rut. Drummond at • length was conceded a free kick which put an end for the time being of the apprehen- sions of the Widnesians. Andrews obtained possession of the ball fram Drummond's kick, but although he made a strong attempt he failed to penetrate through the cordon of defence opposed to him. Barber now get possession and passed to Broady but still the Widnes men were too much alert for any score to be made against them. Apprehensions of a pointless draw began to arise among the Warrington spectators, when Barber

(Widnes) transgressed the new off side rule and thus got his side penalised. Burton had the ball placed

for him in the centre of the field, and by a surprising kick, the ball MU sent sailing over the goal pests, amid a loud outburst of applause. Barber restarted. and Hill made a splendid run dodging a great number of his opponents, hut unfortunately his final pass was a little wild and Barber (Warrington) was unable to catch the ball. The game was going at a great pace. and tackling on both sides was remarkably keen. and Fairhurst, Nevins, Hill and Swift among the for- wards put iii a tremendous amount of work. Great enthusiasm was aroused by a fine round of passing be- tween Houghton, Clark, Barber, Burton, Cross and Broady, and the latter was going off for the line at a great pace when he was grassed.Widnes were repeatedly penalised for off side tactics, but nothing tangibie came of the fine kicks. After a brief visit to the War- rington end, Warrington returned to the attack, and mnch amusement was caused when Frazer was seen with the ball in his possession just on his own line, and Hill like the "old man of the sea" riding upon his shoul- ders. Needless to say, Frazer could make no progress, wth such a burden, and he was promply brought to the ground. Widnes carried the ensuing scrimmage, and for the remainder of the game.the game was much more even, Widnes at times almost equalising. The ball on one occasion was sent beyond Heesom, and a fine race for possession was made. Burton succeeded in getting I the ball near his own line, and quite dumbfounded his pursuers by a backward kick in touch right over his head. The game continued in doubt as to the result right up to the end, but when the whistlejblew. Warr- ington had won a hard-earned victory by a penalty goal (three points) to nil. RUNCORN v. OLDHAM.

At Runcorn. The visitors arrived at Runcorn in fairly good time by special train, accompanied by some 500 supporters. Great care had been taken by Oldham to put their best team on the ground. Etchells, J. Furness, A. Childs, R. Edwards, and J.Lees forwards. offered- his services, and Warder went. into the centre. West, from the A team, going on the wing. The full team was :—J. Butterworth. back: T. Warder. G. Robinson, B. West, and W. Gayter, three-quarters; S. Abrams and J. Faulkner, half has; W. Faulkner, A. Taylor, S. Walker, TI. Farmer, R. Reece, W. Lightfoot, G. Moores and F. Gayter. forwards. Referee, J. Slevin, Wigan. Faulkner started for Rtracorn, and Lees smartly returned. To this West responded, putting the ball well into touch by the centre flag. Play was very keen and exciting. A series of short exchanges by Varley, Lees, and Taylor gave Runcorn some work to check. Taylor a few minutes later got possession, and eluding his opponents went behind the Runcorn goal, Lees kicking a good goal. Faulkner started again, and quickly the home end was visited. The home forwards, however. I got the ball away. This relief was only short, as McCutcheon threw back to Lees, who was allowed to slip away, and taking a drop at goal, shot the ball over the bar las a really fine drop. This reverse roused the home side, who made a determined attack. Abrams gave West a chance to score, but fumbling the ball the open- ing was lost. Still Runcorn kept up the attack. The home forwards frequently got possession. and heeling out Faulkner and Abram. got away. Throwing to Garter, the latter was pushed into touch at the corner flag. Following the throw out Robinson leoked to have a clear run in, but the great speed of the Oldham quartette gave them a great advantage, enabling them to check the attack. The interval ar- rived, Oldham having three goals in hand to Runcorn nothing. After the ;nterval Oldham started, Warder returning. when Whittle taking the ball, raced into the home half, a movement w'Aich was well stopped. The home side than showed some fine passing, in which J. Faulkner, Abrams, Robinson, and West took part, but Eagland saved grandly when they were close up to the line. The advantage gained was well kept 'up, the de- fence of the 04lham men being severely taxed, but all the efforts by the home side to score prove nf no avail. The game for a while was fought in neutral ground, where McCutcheon got an opportunity to break away, and sprinting cleared all except Butterworth, who was ready for his man and effected a very fine tackle. W. Faulkner started a dribble, and was joined by Taylor and Walkar, who worked the ball hack to centre. where Abrams and J. Faulkner tried tn break away and seemed clear, but the fine play of McCutcheon and Lees frus- trated any effort to get over. Near to the close Runcorn had hard lineg, a faulty pass spoiling the chance, and later Varley, by a single-handed effort,broke away,scor- ing an excellent try. Eagland making a good shot at I goal. Tlme.—Oldham by three goals (one dropped) and I one try to Runcorn nothing.

WARRINGTON "A " v. WIDNES "A."—The most for- midable engagement of the season for the Warrington "A " team was that of Saturday, when they met the hitherto unbeaten Widnes "A." Still the visitors did not leaveWarringten with the reeling that they were going to get a record licking. Far from it ! for the team picked for the occasion was the strongest which has yet played. It was composed as follows :—Full back,Spruce ; three- quarters, Foster, Scott, Lawless, and Carey ; half backs, O'Brien and Foden ; forwards, Wilcock, G. Savage, J. Savage, Monks, Hughes,Bailey, Scholtze, and P.Lawless. Widnes : Full back,Grayson : threequarters, Goulding, Alford, Percival, and Wilson; half backs, Douglas and Highfield ; forwards, Forster, Davies, Ashby, Murray, Travis, E. Derbyshire, Hughes,and M'Cabe. The game was played on the new ground,before a moderate num- ber of spectators. Warrington kicked off and the ball wes returned into touch. A line out and scrimmage fol- lowed and from the latter Scott got possession and passed to Foden,who was run into touch near the line. O'Brien and Cari;, worked up well and another strimmage took place near the line, and Carey from loose play kicked at goal, but the ball hit the posts and another scrimmage was held just under goal, with the result that O'Brien got over. Lawless easily converted. The Widnes kick was charged down, and the visitors rushed to the quar- ter flag again. Here Foster picked up the oval and raced for the line which he just managed to fall across as he was tackled. Lawless again converted. After the re-start Widnes got a free kick against Warrington from offside play and gained ground a little. A scrimmage well within Warrington territory put Douglas in possession and he almost got over. Scott and Carey gave Warrington relief and the latter passed to Lawless who made a splendid run, but was tripped up near the line. G. Savage was however close behind and snatching up the ball, scored, while Lawless kicked the goal. Play for a period became uninteresting, though Widnes had the best of matters. They secured another free kick which was mulled, and Carey punteJ into touch. O'Brien stopped a rush by judicious tackling and Foden passed into touch further up field. Lawless secured and kicked to further advantage, and Carey made a neat run, but was grassed. Half-time was then called with the score, Warrineton.3 goals to Widnes nil. With 15 points against them Widnes restarted the game vigorously, and the threeqnarters passing well T. Fors- ter (Widnes) secured and made a god run for the line but was tackle. The homesters followed up their lead, and a scrimmage taking place near the line, Warrington had a warm time of it, but nothing resulted. The game then crossed the field with Widnes still near the line, and being granted a free kick Alford had bard lines in not scoring. Foden gave timely relief, and Scott intercepting a Widnes pass ran up field before being stopped. Marks were then made on both sides and the homesters following up well were checked at a critical moment by Spruce. Lawless and Carey exchanged and the latter, made a good run to mid-field. T. Forster next secured and almost reached the line. A scrimmage ensued and Alford got over, but the goal kick failed. Play after this became somewhat rough the homesters apparently being unable to swallow their beating amic- ably. A pass forward from Carey to Foster gave Widnes a free kick near the goal and Alford put the ball over the bar. Shortly after, time was called leaving Warrington victors of a well-fought game by 3 goals (15 points) to Widnes, 1 (penalty) goal, 1 try (6 points).

WARRINGTON S,T. MARY'S -V.- LONDON AND NORTH- WESTERN Locos.—This match was played on the ground of the former on Saturday. The Saints won the toss. The visitors kicked off, the ball being well returned by Warburton. The Saints pressed, Raybold relieving with a kick to half way. Mason and Hi4ginbottem passed finely from a scrimmage. Chapman bringing Mason down on the line. The Saints still pressed, and the visitors' halves passing in their own 25, Hampson inter- cepted and dashed over, Mason converting. From the kick-off the Locos invaded the home quarters and pressed, but found Roughley safe. The visitors were penalized for infringing the new rule, Hampson making a good bid for goal. Chapman touching down. Gregory secured from the restart, and grand passing by the whole of the forwards made a good opening for Hampson, who scored near the corner, Siddall converting. Half time was shortly afterwards called, the Saints leading by ten points. Thomas restarted for the homesters, who im- mediately pressed. Some nice passing by Powney, Ray- bold, and Sudlow took play to the home" 25," a fine run and kick by Roughley being the means of the Saints again attacking. Mason got away from a scrimmage, and passed to Hampson, who scored his third try,Rough- ley converting. Siddall and Bennett were prominent at the restart, the latter player having hard lines. Chap- man relieved, but the Saints attacked to the end of the game, and play rested in the visitors' quarters when the whistle blew for time.—Result St. Mary's three goals (15 points), Locos nil. Jolley hitting the cross-bar from a drip, the final score being—Oldham, two goals, two tries ; Runcorn one try.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DISORDERLINESS IN SANKEY-ST.

In consequEnce of the numerous complaints which Mr. Talbot, the new Chief Constable, has received from all quarters with regard to the nuisance caused by young people jostling and shouting and making themselves disagreeable on Sunday nights in Sankey-st., he has de- tailed a number of constables to be on duty during that time with orders to report such young people as are disorderly in their behaviour. The consequence has been that three young men, Richard Howard, junior, 16, Phillip-st. ; William Weaver, 18, Bluecoat-at.; and Edward Williams, 10, Chorley-st., were each summoned at the Warrington Borough Court on Monday, before the Mayor and Alderman Harrison, for being disorderly in Sankey-at., on Sunday, December 22.—P.C. Lewis said that on that date he was on duty in Sankey-street, near the end of Bold-st., when he saw the three defend- ants with four more,walking abreast. They were shout- ing, whistling, and jostling the people off the footpath. The three were so disorderly that he had to report them. He had been detailed to look after that sort of thing.— Mr. Talbot : There have been many complaints about this sort of conduct, and they were made to me as soon as I came to the town. I have been in the streets and have seen it personally.—P.C. Lewis (proceeding) said it was a practice of young men to jostle young girls about.—P.C. Barlow and P.C. Lewis each said they had t.) look alter Sankey-st. They had also warned the defendants a short time previous to P.C. Lewis.—The Chief Constable : This is a common thing in many towns. In Southport,they are beginning to send them to gaol without the option of a fine. We shall shortly have a bye-law dealing with these cases.— The Bench decided to bind the defendants over for six months in recognizances of £5, and ordered them to pay the costs, 7s.

YOUTHFUL FOOTBALL AND SHINTY PLAYERS

The following boys were brought before the Warring- ton magistrates on Monday for playing football in Crown-street, on December 29: John Marnon, 1, Packet- street; James Hardman, 17, James-street; Emmanuel James Brown, 28, James-street; Thomas O'Connor, 16, Cockhedge-lade ; Joseph Tavlin, 29. Orford-street; Randalls Knowles, 25, Grford-street ; James Tavlin 29, Orford-street; Abel Whittaker, 11, Naylor-street; and Charles Buck, 6, James-street. P.C. Evans proved the case, and said that many complaints had been received from various quarters about this practice. The Chief Constable said that playing football in the streets was past a nuisance, it was really a disease. Everything the youngsters saw they kicked. The defendants were all fined is. and threatened with severer penalties if they offended again.—The following : Joseph Gerard. 4,Bake- house-yard ; Frank Leyland, 56, Golborne-street ; John Shaw, 50, Golborne-street ; William Bailey, jun., 1, Golden Grove-street ; Henry Bennett, Queen-street ; James Barber. 3, Cambria Place ; James Jackson, 43. King-street; Peter Finch, 11, Foundry-street; and George Littlewood, 26. Kitig,-street, were all charged with play- ing shinty in Queen-street, on December 30th, to the 'annoyance of passengers. P.C. Evans also proved this case. The mother of one of the boys said that it had been holiday at sob 01, and there was nowhere in War- rington where the boys could play without doing wrong. —The Chief Constable said that there was a certain amount of reason in what the woman had said. There were very few open spaces to play shinty.—Each defen- dant was fined is.—Another batch: John Fogg, 79, Birchall-street ; Henry Fogg ; James King, jun., 57. Hamilton-street; Richard King ; and William McMutrie, 61, Hamilton-street, were also fined is. each with the exception of John Fogg, who was fined 2s. 6d., as he had been at the court before,for playing shinty in llamilton- street, on December 31st.

A" CABBY "FINED.

Patrick Tigue, cab driver for Mr. Kiernan, was charged with not having his lamp lighted on December 27th, early in the morning. Inspector Taylor gave par- ticulars of the offence, and Tigue in defence said that he had a new wick in the lamp and it kept going out. He was told to be more careful in future, and was fined in the costs ss.

FOND OF LIQUORS.

At the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday, before the Mayor, and Alderman Harrison, Michael Doyle, and John Travis were charged with • breaking into the pre- mises of Robert Brandreth, off license helder,B Pinner's Brow, sometime between Saturday night and Sunday morning and stealing or receiving knowing to have been stolen five pint bottles of ale.and one bottle of pale stout. The Chief Constable said that in order that the ease might be tried at that court, he would reduce the charge to one of simple larceny. The complainant occupied a lock up shop on Pinner's Brow and displayed in his window bottles of stout and beer. On Saturday night 'le locked up his shop and went to his residence in Man- chester-road. At about 230 rn Sunday morning the police discovered that a pane of glass had been broken in the shop and a number of the bottles had been taken away. A person named Richard Bamford informed the police that he had seen the two prisoners sharing the bottles in Silver-square, and P.C. Evans on this informa- tion, arrested the two. When charged, the prisoner Travis made a statement which implicated himself and the other prisoner. The empty bottles were found near to the residence of the two prisoners.—ln reply to the Mayor,the chief constable said that there were no:shutters to the shop, and his Worship addre.sing the complainant said that he ought to be more careful with his shop,as it amounted almost to leading people into temptation. —The bench decided to fine the two prisoners 10s. and costs each, or in default 14, days. A ViOLENT LODOER.—TWISTING A WOMAN'S ARM.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Tuesday, before Mr. J. H. Fleming and Dr. Smith, Edward Coyne, an ex- army reserve man, was charged on s warrant with as- saulting and beating Bridget Collier, Dutton-at., on the 4th January.—Mrs. Collier, who appeared with her left arm in a sling and much swollen, said that on Saturday night about 10.30 prisoner, who was her lodger,came in and asked for supper. Complainant's sister told him it was not ready and he used very foul language. Witness remonstrated, and he seized her by the hair and dragged her off the sofa on to the floor, where he knelt on her chest. Her husband interfered and prisoner set upon him and gave him two black eves. She went to the table to get something to defend herself with when prisoner seized her wrist and twisted her arm round. She had to go to the Infirmary where the doctor said the shoulder was fractured and the bone displaced. Prisoner was not drunk.—Sarah Elizabeth Bicklev gave corroborative evi- dence. She was sister of complainant,and saw prisoner twist the latter's arm and thump her in the face.—Pri- sonar for defence stated that complainant was "boozing" five days out of the week. She was drinking and pawn- ing goods for drink when he and her husband were al- most starving. On the night in question she attacked prisoner with a knife, and in the scuffle he pushed her against the slepgtone,which fell down,he thought,on her arm.—Kate Smith said on the same night she tor.k com- plainant home drunk. In the row which followed pri- soner nushed her against the slopstone.—ln answer to the Chief Constable she admitted cohabiting with pri- semen—Prisoner had only been prosecuted twice before and the Bench therefore dealt leniently with him in inflicting a penalty of 20s. and costs, or in default one month's hard labour.

HOLYTAKE v. Sr. ELPHIN'S.--Played at Holylake on Saturday. After the kick off the Saints at once showed that they meant business, attacking the home goal in fine style, and after about twenty minutes play J. Duck- worth ran finely down the wing and centred to Dugdale who sent in a rasping shot from the 25 yards line, which appeared to be two yards through the goal when the keeper saved it. The referee, however, disallowed the goal, but Turner, from full back, made amends with a splendid shot from long range, which gave the home custodian no chance. This was the only score till half- time. On the re-start both sides played well, and a grand fight was witnessed, but Thompson, seeing an oppor- tunity, promptly put the ball iuto the net for the Saints, Hickinbottom following it up with another. Time was drawing to a close, and Holylatre, playing desperately, managed to scrimmage a goal through, the Saints also scoring another disallowed goal. The final score read— St. Elphin's throe, Hoylake one.

OUR LOCAL CIIIIRCHES-WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.

On Sunday morning, at W ycliffe Cnurch, was held the annual Young Peoples' service, and in response to the pastor's special invitation to former members of his class the service was largely attended. Special hymns were sung with great heartiness and before going on with his sermon the Rev. John Yonge said in connection with the occasion that morning he hoped he would not be considered formal if he wished everyone there a " Happy New Year." He would like also before taking up his text to give a word of welcome that morning to several former scholars of his class and of the Sunday School, who were present. He was glad that so many had responded to the special invitation he had sent out during the week. It was a matter of some significance he thought, that during the years which the class had been held, over 400 young people had been through its teaching, and he would intensely like as the years passed that the young people of Wycliffe who came in the ranks of that class,should rally round it on New Year's Sunday morning and show that they still retained an affection and regard for the place where they were brought up. The text of the rev. gentleman's discourse that morn- ing was" And the children of Judah prevailed because they relied upon the Lord God of their Fathers," 2 Chron., xiii..lB. The Old Testament, he said, was full of reverence like that and they would always find God spoken of by the Jewish people as,theGod of their fathers. The God of their fathers was the only true and living God and therefore they were to be loyal and faithful to Him. That was a point worth all their most careful thought. In these days many young people, he feared, were getting very heedless about the religion of their fathers. When they attained 15 or 18 years of age they became wonderfully independent of all those matters and began to think that their fathers' way was not worth treading in ; and although they knew their father had always attended the House of God and reverenced it these young people, many of them, think that they would just have their own way in the matter and come or stay away as it suited them. Some young people were getting to be very doubtful about the old Book. They had an idea that it was intellectual and clever, and very refined and advanced to have doubts, and to dis- pute the word of the good old Book. The rev, gentle- man urged upon them afresh the duty and privilege,the blessedness and safety of cleaving fast to the God of their fathers and the religion of their mothers. Many of them, if not all, had been brought up in Christian homes, and were familiar with the name of God and Jesus Christ from their earliest memory. They should understand that they had not been sent out to life unprepared or knowing nothing about the dangers of life and the temp- tations a the world. The Bible had been put into their hands to shine upon the darkness and light up the road. The dangers and difficulties of life were too many for us alone. We should thus stand ready ; neither under-rate our foes nor over-rate them. Avoid both extremes. The children of Judah prevailed because they relied upon the Lord God of their fathers. They did not rely upon. force of arm or strategy, but conquered by their reliance. They should recollect that the strongest people in his. tory had always been those who feared God and abided by his commandments. The foes and temptations in the path of young people were strong, but they should re- member that God was stronger. They should put their reliance upon Him and though all the world were in arms abroad they would dwell in full possession and come off more than conquerors through Him that loves us. At the end of the service the distributioa of Bibles to the scholars of the pastor's class during the past year, took place. 39 scholarr of 15 years of age received a Bible accompanied with a few commendatory words from Mr. Yang°.
