RUNCORN SAVINGS' BANS.

ANNUAL MEETING.

On Wednesday afternoon, the annual meeting of the. trustees and managers of the Rnncorn Savings' Bank was hald at the Savings' Bank. High-street. Dr. McDougall was voted to the chair, and there were also present, Messrs. James Handley, J.P., A. Morris, W. Hankinson, J. Littler and Mr. W. Handley (actuary). Mr. Handley in his annual report said that, as was to. well known, Runcorn had, especially during the past year, been passing through a period of unexampled depression. It would have been remarkable if the Sav- ing' Bank had escaped its natural. effect. Though the amount of deposits had shown a diminution, this had been more than counterbalanced by a smaller amcunt having been withdrawn, and at the CIO3O of the bank ' year the amount due to depositors had increased by £1,731 163. The number of sep irate transactions, deposits and withdrawals was 3,134 representing an amount including sales of consul stock and dividends received of. £16,714 53. 21. As com- pared with the year previous the number of accounts showed a diminution of five only. The classification of the accounts was a 3 follows :- 1039 individval accounts,amounting to £27,093 13s Ild.; 2 penny banks, £9 63. 11d. •, 17 charitable and clothing' societies, £566 Ils. 11d. ; 59 friendly and tontine,£s,39o- - Bd. ; total, £33,060 is. sd. This was, he believed, the largest annual balance standing to the credit of de- positors during the 53 years' existence of the bank, and might be taken as a satisfactory evidence of the general thriftness and provident habits of a considerable pro- portion of the inhabitants of toe town and district. lb would materially conduce, not only to the prosperity of the bank as an institution, but also to the public advan- tage if still greater use was made of the facilities offered by the bank for saving small sums, and their ready withdrawal in case Gf need, by an increase to the num- ber of depositors,and the consequent total in the amount deposited. The whole of the sum due to the depositors with the exception of the amount in the hands of the treasurer needed for current withdrawals, was held by the Government as represented by the National Debt Commissioners. During the year the officials appointed under the Savings' Bank Ants,hed twice attended at the bank and examined the whole of the b.)oks and the system adopted in keeping the bank accountt. In the month of March, to the great regret of the trustees and managers, Mr. Simpson expressed a desire to be relieved of his duties as treasurer, after a service of nearly 40 years. After full conslderation, Mr. J. B. Holland wag appointed to the position with the approval of his direc- tors and the sanction of the National Debt Commis- sioners. Mr. Simpson continued his services as a trustee, a position he has continually held since the establishment of the bank in March, 1842. The auditors in their report stated that they had audited the books and vouchers of the bank and found them to be correct.

The Chairman in moving the adoption of the report said he was sure they regretted the circumstances under which he took the chair that day. They all regretted the absence of Mr. Brundrit who had taken an active- part for many years in connection with that bank, and who was its president and also the oldest depositor. Referring to the report he said it was very gratifying to note that the withdrawals had beert so exceedingly small notwithstanding the crisis through which their town had been passing. There w.as an important point for small dep,sitors in the readness with which they could withdraw their Say- ings, and he would call their attention to the satisfac• tory and able condition of the bank which was owing to the admirable manner it was conductei. He hoped that those who were not. depositers,and who were unable ts have an ordinary banking account, would see their way to become depositors at the Savings' bank. The more widely the usefulness of the bank became known the more would the public take advantage of it. The bank was fortunate in having the discriminating eyeof a government official constantly over the books of the bank and his wide knowledge of figures of that descrip- tion must always be to the advantage of the bank. (Ap•

plause.) Mr. James Handley seconded the adoption of the re- port, and concurred in wh, t had been said regarding the absence of the president of the bank,who had taken such an active share in the management. during the whole period of its existence,now over 50 years. He called at- tention to the gratifying fact that there were over 1,10. depositors, and that their deposits amounted tc) over £30,000, which gave an average of about £3O each. (Hear,hear.) In addition to that there was another fea- ture which was equally gratifying, and that was that small depositors were beginning to take advantage of the facilities which the Government had placed at their disposal for investing their money in consols for which they were able to obtain a fixed rate of dividend, and also able to withdraw their money at any time when circumstances required. Those were the advantages of the way y andtbe business that the wp savings' banks for those who availed themselves of them.. He was glad that their savings' bank was not only hold. public entitcwteci d e itnbge irt an appreciation autti nn progressing, Th by increasing the deposits. He had very great pleasure in seconding the adoption of the report. On the motion of the Chairman. Mr. J. B. Holland, the hon. treasurer of the bank was thanked for his ser- vices. The Actuary read a letter from Mr. Edward Marwood

who tendered-his resignation as one of the auditors oa account of family bereavement and other circumstances. —Mr. Littler moved the following.' resolution : " La ac- cepting with regret and sympathy the resignation of Marwood as auditor the trustees and managers hereby place on record their high appreciation of his valuable services to the bank for many years." He said he had known Mr. Mar wood for many years and had alwayS found him a conscientious worker. --Mr. James Handley seconded,and said they all sympathised with Mr. Mar- wood in the circumstances which had led him to resign. —The resignation was carried unanimously. Mr. J. Littler proposed that the following be the auditors :—Messr. J. H. Salkeld, W. Williamson, C. Pownall, and W. B. Kay.—Mr. Morris seconded, and they were elected. Mr. James Flandley proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman.—Mr. Morris seconded, and it was agreed to. —Dr. McDougall responded, and hoped that every per son would put their shoulder to the wheel and try to bring about an improved state of things in the town. The meeting then terminated.

THE ORDER Or ANCIENT SEIEPHERDEft On Saturday afternoon the quarterly meeting of the Warrington district of this order was held at the Cheshire Cheese Inn, Latchford. The chairman was Mr. T. C. Duckworth, and the vice-chairman Mr. William Eckersley, who was supported by the treasurer (Mr. Thomas Parr). Mr. Edgar (secretary), and 20 other delegates. The secretary read the statement of accounts and the auditor's report, which were considered satisfactory, and were passed. The usual routine of the, quarterly business was then gone through, and at the conclusion of this'a short discussion took place upon several mat- ters referred to in the A. M. C. report of the Order. A vote of thanks to the chairman brought the meeting to a close.

AT WARRINGTON.

" The general usefulness of.the Parr Hall was fur- ther exemplified on Thursday week,w'nen the annual Old People's Treat was held there. The tables were arranged parallel with the corridors and when everything was in full swing,presented an interest- ing spectacle, The rows of grey heads—occasion- ally relieved by a bald one—looked both odd and striking, but there was no mistaking the happiness of the faces below them. Nearly 500 men, 60 years of age and upwards, were entertained to a substan- tial dinner, contisting of roast beef, roast pork, roast mutton, vegetables of various kinds, beer. plum-pudding, and bread and cheese. The following members of the Old Men's Dinner and Aged Women's Tea Committee were present : Alderman Sutton (president), Mr. Thomas Morris (hon. treasurer), Alderman Wright and Mr. T. A. Sutton (joint hon. secretaries), and Messrs. Peter Chrimes, James Smethurst, David Fanshaw. Daniel Jones, George Pilling, Harry Toft, Alfred Woods and Arthur Browne, to whom much praise is due for the great trouble which the preparation of the dinner had involved, all the details having been most sat- isfactorily carried out. Among others present were the May Dr and Mayoress (Councillor and Mrs. James Fairclough), Miss Mabel Fairclough,the Rev. Canon Willis, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, the Rev. H. V. and Mrs. Pigot, Mesdames Joseph Harrison, James Smethurst, Daniel Jones, A:fred Woods, James Richardson, George Holford, W. J. Forshaw, the Misses Spinks, Smethurst, Johnson (Penketh). Gill, Westwell, the Town Clerk (Mr. J. L. Whittle), Councillors Dr. Cannel! and John Lee, Dr. Peacocke, Mr. and Mrs. T. Shackleton, and Messrs. George Watkins, W. Gill,Frank H. Aukland, John Harrison, J. H. Smethurst, H. Hoult, T. Amos, J. Birtles, J. W. Haynes, Brundrett, Wright. jun., Francomb, jun., Taylor and Sutton, jun. The ladies headed by the Mayoress. were very zealous in their efforts to see everyone was served promptly and well, while their endeavours were ably supplemented by a number of young gentlemen. During the course of the dinner Mr. W. S. Woods played selections on the organ. On the completion of the meal and after the distribution of pipes and tobacco, the Mayor, Alderman Sutton, and several members of the committee ascended the platform. The PRESIDENT, Alderman Sutton, wished the old men the customary season's greetings and extended to them a hearty welcome from the committee. He observed ' that there was every indication that there was going to be better trade and more prosperous times, and he hoped that all the old men present who were fit to work would be able to find employment during the ensuing year. The MAYOR said he was pleased to have another oppor- tunity of seeing the old men at their dinner and in such a comfortable room. Last year they met in the Drill Hall, but since then Mr. Parr had generously presented the Parr Hall to the town and they were very grateful to him. (Hear, hear and applause.) He only wished Mr. Parr could have been present to see the old men, it would have rejoiced his heart exceedingly. He concluded by calling for three cheers for the committee, which were heartily given. Songs were at intervals rendered by Mr. George Bar- ton, the well-known Lancashire tenor, and Mr. J. S. Haynes gave a couple of much-appreciated numorous recitations, "The Charge of the Light Brigade," and "The Schoolgirl's Recitation." At five o'clock, tea consisting of sandwiches, currant bread, bread and butter, celery, Szc., was served to the old women, who numbered nearly 703, and completely filled the ground floor of the hall. The eatables disposed of all waited expectantly for the commencement of the entertainment.

'There was a very large attendance at 7 o'clock when an excellent variety entertainment was given. Down- stairs the building was crowded by the 700 women who sat for the tea and who now remained in the same places to enjoy the entertainment. On the platform were a number of members of the committee and also the Mayor. Alderman Sutton (president of the committee) officiated as chairman and in opening the proceedings said that on behalf of the committee he desired to give them all a hearty welcome and wish them a happy New Year. (An- plause.)He supposed they had something like 700 or 800 old ladies present that night who had •partaken of tea and about 400 men who partook of dinner, and the wonder was where they all came from (Laughter). Warrington could net be such an unhealthy town when they could produce so many old people of 60 years and upwards. (Hear, hear, and applause.) They all had a deal to be thankful for—in being allowed to meet there another Fear and also for the fact that instead of being bandied about from first one place and then another they had through the munificence of Mr. Parr—(applause) that magnificent Hall to assemble in. They were also thankful to all those good peple of the town who had subscribed to the proceedings. it was impossible to particularise but he might mention :that the other day Mr. John Crosfield sent them £5 (applause) ; ano'her gentleman who had been kind to them in the past and had this time given every old woman a present of a packet of tea and sugar, was Mr. T. Fletcher. (Ap- plause.) The MAYOR said that they had all been wished a Happy New Year and he would therefore wish them many Happy Returns of the Day." (Applause.) He hoped that they had all enjoyed their tea, and by their beaming faces lie judged they had. (Laughter and ap- plause.) He concluded by asking them to give a hearty round of applause to the president of the committee, the vice-president, treasurer,and secretaries who had got up the entertainment which was no light task for one day. —The tribute to the labours of these gentlemen was then boisterously accorded. Mr. T. MORRIS said that the treat to the old men and women cost something like £7O or £BO each year and unless he got £BO he could not pay the expenses of this one. Mr. Sutton, the president, wrote to the papers a few days ago asking wealthy people to assist more gener- ously.but he got very few replies. He mentioned that they had now only abont £l9 in the bank and would require £7O or £BO before commencing to collect for the nest treat and he appealed to their friends in the gallery and others, to assist them willingly. The entertainment then commenced and the following items were rendered including humorous songs by Mr. Frank Lelsdham :—" Death of Nelson," Mr. George Bar- ton ; "Darby and Joan," Madame Parker ; dialect reci- tation,"Come whoam to the childer an' me," Mr. Dennis Davies; "Mona," Mr. George Barton. After an interval val Madame Parker sang " Dear Heart" ; Mr. Leedham gave another humorous sketch ; Mr. Barton sang "Nora, the Pride of Kildare "; and Mr. Davies gave an excellent delivery of "Poor Old Noddle." This concluded the vocal part of the entertainment which terminated with a most laughable nigger farce entitled "Black Justice," in which the characters were taken as follows :—Judge, Mr. J. S. Haynes ; Counsel, Mr.W.C. Litton; Policeman, Mr.G.H. Hoult ; and minor parts were takes by Messrs. F. H. Aukland, Harold Loft, and W. Hoult. Mr. W. H. Payton accompanied on the piano. The whole proceed- ings proved highly enjoyable, and a thorough treat to the old folks,while the occupants of the gallery were not lacking in applause at various points. During the interval Alderman Sutton announced that the total number of men who came to dinner was 480 against 600 last year. The decrease he attributed to better trade and less depression:The highest age amor get the men was 89. 690 women appeared, making 90 more than last year. The eldest was 95. The grand total of both sexes was 1,170. Packets of tea and sugar, an orange and mince pie were then given to each woman. COCKREDGE MISSION. A DISPUTE OVER A RAFFLE TICKET.

A singular claim was heard at the Warrington County Ccurt on Thursday, before his Honour judge Wynne Ftoulkes, in which Joseph Bradbury, puddler, 75, Well- field-st., brought an action against John Wooton, iron worker, 108 Cartwright-st., for the specific return of a raffle ticket which he alleged had been obtained from him by the defendant by means of a trick. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of the plaintiff,and Mr.T. H. Granger defended.—Mr. Browne said that some time ago his client purchased a raffle ticket from a man named Thomas Morris, and it was numbered 38,and the article to be raffled for was a gun which was at Huyton Quarry. The purchase took place in the Bowling Green lrin,and a friend of the plaintiff named Samuel Steven- son was also present and bought number 37. The gun was raffled for a few weeks ago, and shortly afterwards defendant visited plaintiff's house and asked what was the number of his ticket. The plaintiff replied " 38." Wooton then asked him to lend him the ticket and he would see if it was the winner. Bradbury did so, and Wooton took it away with him. Suteequently Brad- bury discovered that the winning number was 38, and that therefore he had won the gun. He accordingly went to the defendant and asked him for the ticket say • ing that he wanted to get the gun. Defendant however, refused to give up the ticket on the plea that it had not been paid for. Plaintiff replied that he had paid Thomas Morris for it, but to that Wooton answered that he had not received the shilling from Morris, and that he would not give the ticket up before Morris gave him the shill- ing.Mr.Browne put in a letter purporting to be from the defendant to Mr. S. Merrington, of Huyton Quarry, having reference an the tickets which he said Morris had had up to the previous week. Be had seen Morris about the tickets and had also spoken to" Jack Williams about them." The latter would not take them because he had others to get rid of. " Tom Morris has got two books to sell, or else he would have had the lot. The other six he has sold, and I have to see him again for the money which he says will be all right. I told him that what tickets he had left I would send them hack to you. J. WOOTON."—After the letter had been read, the Judge asked who had got up the lottery,but Mr. Browne replied that he did not know. It was not the defendant, how- ever.—Mr. Granger at this point asked if the case did not come under the Gaming Act, but his Honour said that it did not, as it was merely for the regaining pos- session of a piece of paper and had nothing to do with the lottery itself. He would advise a friendly settlement of the affair.—This course was not adopted, and the plaintiff was then called. He corroborated his solicitor's statement, and in cross-examination by Mr. Granger, he denied that he had had the ticket from Wooton. He stated emphatically that it was from Morris that he had bought the ticket, and that he had paid for it at the same time.—Thomas Morris, shingler at Bewsey Forge, deposed ta selling the ticket to the plaintiff and to re- ceiving a shilling in payment. The raffle had been got up, at Huyton Quarry, and some tickets had been given to him to get rid of. Having sold some of them, he handed the remaining ones over to the defendant. He also offered him the shilling which he had received foom the plaintiff, but Wooton replied that he weuld rather receive the whole of the money at once, In his (witnees)opinion,Wooton had refused the money because he wanted the gun himself.—Samuel Stevenson also gave corroborative testimony.—Mr. Granger said that his case was that the tickets had been in charge of Wooton and that they had been handed over by him to Morris on October 22nd to sell. Morris subsEquently handed him back the tickets he had not sold, and it was then that Brodbury asked for one. One was given to him, Bradbury promising to pay for it later on. A day sr two later, defendant met Brad oury and ;asked him for the money,but the latter replied that he had paid Morris. Defendant then said that he had not authorised Morris to receive the money AO that it had not been properly paid for. In conclusion, Mr. Granger submitted that if there was a contract in the first place for the sale and pur- chase of the ticket, it was voluntarily discharged when the plaintiff handed back the ticket to Wooton rather than pay for R.—John Wooton, the defendant, was then called,and said that 12 tickets had been sent to a person named John Williams to sell at Dallam Forge, but as he w nild not have them, he (witness) took charge of them and subsequently handed them to Morris to sell. A week afterwards Morris asked him to take the ticket hack,and said that Bradbury had one, and promised to pay the shilling later on. Bradbury afterwards told witness that he had paid Morrie for the ticket. He could not get the money and the consequence was that defendant had to pay the money for the ticket himself. In fact Bradbury returned :the ticket rather than pay for it.---Cross examined : He admitted that he had claimed the gun from Huyton Quarry. He denied that Morris had Ever offered him the money for the ticket, and also discoun- tenanced the letter handed in by Mr. Browne, saying that he had not written it, rer had anything to do with ic.—His Honour said that the weight of evidence was with the plaintiff, and he believed that the ticket had been paid for by Bradbury. He would, therefore, give judgment for the specific return of the ticket, and also Is. damages, and ordered payment of costs.

THE JACKSON-HARMS WORTH POLAR EXPEDITION.

A very interesting souvenir of the Jackson Harms. worth Polar Expedition is at ores.nt beim, exhibited by a well-known firm of Queen's Furriers in Bold-st., Liver- pool. This is the skin of a magnificent Polar bear which fell to the unerring aim ot Mr. F. G. Jackson, F.R.G.B , who is in command of the expedition. This splendid specimen from the polar region, was forwarded by Mr. Jackson to hi; brother by the Windward, which brought home tidings of the intrepid explorers. Tha reason why the skin was handed to Messrs. W. Creamer and Co. for treatment is that our famous North of England firm of furriers are connected in a very practical:way with the expedition, inasmuch as they supplied the whole of the suits of fur clothing and sleeping bags which Mr. Jack- son took with him on his pioneer polar attempt. Messrs Creamer most certainly he congratulated upon the com- pliment which has been paid to them by Mr. Jackson, and the promoters of the expedition, for it unquestion- ably indicates in a very marked manner the national eminence and wide-reaching reputation they have attained.

In 1694 France obtained over sixty thousand pounds from the taxation of bicycles. The number of wheels " declared " was one hundred and forty seven thousand nine hundred and seventy-seven. The amount received from the tax was nearly five times that obtained during the same period from the taxation of horses and other animals used for drawing vehicles.

/Mao to the ettitor.

COUNCILLOR BENNETT ON TLIE UNEM- PLOYED AND IRELAND. Sir,—lt would probably be a sufficient reply to Mr. J. C. Dalton's letter to refer him to mine of the 19th December last. I have already answered him twice— once at the Debating Society, where the matter ought to have ended, and once through the columns of "The Examiner," as above mentioned; and i'; is too much to expect me to answer him every other week. However, 41 order that there may not be any possibility of mis- understanding, permit me to say again : First, that I never used thelword of which Mr. Dalton complains in any such sense as he attach.s to it, and meant it to be applied to the problem to which I was alluding and not to the unfortunate peopth who are affected by it. Secondly, that the whole tenour of my observations showed plainly that they were prompted by a strong sympathy with the unemployed, and a desire to find them employment. Thirdly, that, in my reply at the close of the debate, so far from" making indecent remarks about the poor and the afflicted," I said that the heart of every humane man wassaddened by the poverty and the distress which unfortunately prevail in our great cities, that it was our imperative duty to endeavour to deal with the question; and that Imperial Federation offered two ways of doing this—l mentioned others as well—one of which was to endeavour to restore -and to increase our commercial prosperity by establishing Free Trade with the Colonies, and the other by permanently providing any who might still be unsuccessful in obtaining satisfactory employ- ment at home with the opportunity of receiving new homes—not in the workhouse—but in those noble British Colonies where the old flag floats, and the old language 'a spoken,and the old laws revered, and where there are ample opportunities for everybody, on lands which are the richest in the world. Feurthly, that, in everything that I have ever said or written on the subject, it has been plain to my unpreju- diced mind that' have always regarded the"problems of poverty" as amongst the saddest and the most impor- tant of any of the problems with which we have to deal : and that, if there is one thing more than another which distinguishes " the New Conservatism," it is its sympathy with social legislation, and its desire to brighten the lives ol the poor. I may add that, for over fifteen years, I have been actively connected with Debating Societies, but that. during all that time, Mr. Dalton is the first person who has ever. to the best of my remembrance, refused to ac- cept an explanation offered in good faith in the course of a discussion, and has preferred, for party reasons, to drag the matter into the press. I have now explained my views three times,but,if Mr. Dalton thinks that he can damage me in the eyes of my constituents, or serve even the Home Rule cause, by again repeating his misrepresentation, well, England is a free country,and there is not anything to prevent him, but he must pardon me for remarking that such a method of warfare is not usual amongst fair-minded people.—Faithfully yours, Warrington, 2nd January, 1896.

ARTHUR BENSETT.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday. Mr. William Houghton presided, and there were also present Messrs. J. Houghton, J. Warburton, J. Dignum, T. Halton, J. W. Knowles. J. Mather, J. Johnson, S. Sutton (clerk), Dr. Sephton (medical officer), F. Kerfoot (nuisance inspector), and D. S. Stone (representing Mr. Owen).

ILLNESS OF THE CHAIRMAN AND SIIRTRYOR.

A letter was received from the Rev. G. F. Roberts ex- pressing his regret at not being able to be present owing to illness.—The CHAIRMAN said that they all very much regretted the cause of Mr. Roberts' absence, and hoped that he would soon be able to be with them again.—The CLBEZ said that Mr. Owen, the surveyor, was also away owing to a temporary illness, and that he was repre- sented at that meeting by Mr. Stone.

===== 0002601_18960111_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
BALANCE IN HAND.

The CLERK reported that the balance in hand was as follows: General account, £7l 12g. 7d.; and Burton- wood sewerage loan account, £1,280 51.

TSB REV. J. S. LANGHAM AND THE COUNCIL.

With reference to the matter in dispute between the Rev. J. J. Langham and the Council concerning the case of a child of the former which had been in the Fever Hospital and which he wished the Council to pay for, the Clerk said that the Town Clerk of Warrington had advised that the Council pay the bill and then recover from the Rev. J. J. Langham.—Mr. KNOWLES said that that was the wrong course. They could have no claim against Mr. Langham if they paid the bill. Referring to letters which Mr. Langham had written, Mr. Knowles said that they ought not to enter upon any paper war- fare with that gentleman. He described Mr. Langham's letter to the press as an insulting letter, and he did not think it wri far from libellous, but he supposed they could not collectively take any action against him. The CLERK suggested that a letter be sent to the Town Clerk setting forth their answer to the allegations of Mr. Langham, and the Council decided to adopt this course. 'MI PROPOSED GAS LAMP AT PADGATE RAILWAY STATION. A letter was read from Mr. T. H. Granger, clerk to the Poulton-with-Fearbead Parish Council, calling the atten- tion of the District Council to the fact that the lamp which was promised to be put at the entrance to Pad. gate station had not vet been placed there.—The CLERIC said that it was not the fault of the District Council that the lamp had not been erected. The gas department of the Corporation had been so busy that they had not found time to de the work. As soon as the pressure of business had abated, the work would be done.—Mr. Jomi HOUGHTON : It will soon be light enought to do without a lamp.—lt was decided that the Clerk write to the gas department asking for the work to be done at once.

CLAIM EY A FARMER FOR COMPENSATION. A claim for compensation was sent to the Council by Mr. Matthew Pownall, farmer, Sankey, for loss he alleked he had suffered by a sewage pipe being placed across his field to take the sewage of Mr. Tunstall's house lower down the brook which forms the boundary between the townships of Great Sankey and Penkettc. The Clerk said that a complaint was made some years ago by Mr. Pownall that the sewage from Mr. Tunstall's house polluted the brook from which his (Mr. Pownall's) cows drank. He therefore asked that the sewage be de- posited further down the brook,and nearer the river. It was ultimately decided to place a sewage pipe which had to go through a field belonging to Mr. Pownall,and that gentleman now claimed that he had suffered loss to his pasture to the extent of some £5 which a Mr. Hunt,who rented the field off Mr. Pownall claimed,and Mr.Pownall also claimed 30s. for himself. He (the clerk) said that he must say that some compensation would have to be paid to Mr. Pownall, but he did not see that they had anything to do with Mr. Hunt.—Mr. Pownall, who appeared in person to make, his claim, was then called in, and produced the vsluation which the burley- man of Penketh had placed upon the damages suffered by the work of laying the sewage pip-,which was laid at £5. A long discussion arose between Mr. Pownall, the members of the Council,and Mr. Pownall was then re- quested to leave the room while the matter was discussed in private.. It was finally decided that Mr. Pownall be allowed 30s. for the loss he had suff-red personally, but the Council declined to entertain the claim of £5 which was made by Mr. Hunt. It was stated by Mr. Kerfoot that Mr. Hunt raised no objection to the men going on the field for the purpose of laying the pipe. ANOTHER CLAIM AGAINST THE COUNCIL. A letter was read from Mr. S. Wallhead on behalf of Mr. W. D. Houghton complaining that the brook above mentioned was Polluted with sewage owing to it being discharged only 250 yards away ,rom a'reservoir which Mr. Houghton used for the purpose of his works. Mr. Wallhead also alleged that Mr.Houghton had the right of abstraction of water from the bro.- k, and threatened legal proceedings if steps were not taken at once to meet this complaint. —The Council passed a resolution autho- rising the Clerk to obtain legal assistance should Mr. Wallhead's threat be carried out, and the right of Mr. Houghton to a public watercourse was also ques- tioned.

THE TAXING OF BICYCLES. The Newhaven District Council sent a communica- tion recommending that the Warrineton Council join with them in favour of putting a tax upon bicycles, the tax to be used for the improvement of highways.—Mr. Beroweits said that he understood that the -Runcorn District Council had passed a resolution in fa- vour of this proposal, and he did not see why they should not do so also. RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The last meeting of the old Board was held in the Board room at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. J. Pritchard occupied the chair, and there were also present :—Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McDougall, Mr. F. J. Norman,J.P., Mr. E. J.P., and Mr. G. Moss (clerk.

INCREASE OF SALARIES. The School Management Committee recommended that the salary of W. Sennan, certificated assistant teacher at the Greenway-road Boys' School be advanced to £9O per annum, commencing February Ist, 1896. Tne com- mittee also recommended that the salary of W. Wilson, assistant teacher at Brunswick-street boys' school be advanced to £65 per annum, commencing February Ist. —Dr. McDougall moved the adoption of the minutes, and stated that the increase of salaries came in due course, and was in accordance with the custom of the Board.—Mr. Norman seconded the adoption of the minutes.—The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi moved an amend- ment that the question of increase of salaries be deferred for six months. He did so on the ground that unless they did so, they would have to rescind one of the propositions adopted when they took over Granville-street and Brunswic street schools, namely that they were transferred in statu quo and that no change should take place until they were established in the new schools to he erected. They were going too fast in advancing salaries of those already well paid for their services,and were doing an act against the ratepayer's interests. The town was in a state of despondency, almost bankruptcy he might call it. At almost every meeting of the Board there was the ques- tion of increases of salary. Be proposed that the matter be deferred for six months.—Mr. unison said it was clear that Dr. Gastaldi's argument did not apply to one of the teachers who was not affected by the resolution which Dr. Gastaldi had mentioned. With regard to the other teacher, he thought as practical men they had to consider that the advance was only the marketable value of his services. If they were called upon to fill up the vacancy they would have to pay the amount they proposed to give him. To the latter argument of the previous speakers, that the question of salaries was always recurring at their meetings, he might say that it always would until they had drawn up a scale of salaries.—Dr. McDougall said the committee had broken no faith but had merely done what they considered the teacher in question could claim, that was to be put on an equal footing with the other assistants of the Board schools, who were exactly on a par with himself in point of attendance and ability to teach. They did not pro- pose to interfere in the least degree with Use policy of the Board when these schools were taken over. The question was recognised when he applied for an increase of salary that it would be better to place him on an equal footing with others rather than to place him in a posi- tion of discontent. He hoped Dr. Gastaldi weuld with- drawn his observation. Nelther the committee nor any one connected with the Board was prepared to go behind what had been decided upon.—Dr. Gastaldi stated that Dr. Mc Dougall was one of those who stated that they must have nothing to do with increases of salaries in regard to the Wesreyan schools which the Beard had taken over until they had settled down, and were able to see their own way. He thought it was very premature to increase salaries with the fact before them that nothing should be done until they had got the schools In proper order.—Dr. McDougall said that was not a proper interpretation of what the Board meant. The agree- ment with the teachers was to be of a temporary char- acter until the policy of the Board had developed itself, and they saw what salaries were required. The teacher was entitled to the increase if the schools had gone on under the old management, and they felt they could not resist the application.—As no one seconded Dr. Gas- taldi's amendment it fell to the ground. THE PROPOSED INCREASE OF MEMBERS.—DEFERRED BY THE EDUCATION DEPARTMENT.

A communication was read from the Education De- partment stating that as the triennial election of the School Board has taken place, their Lordships could not now issue an order for the election of two additional mem- bers of the School Board. But their Lordships will in the order for the next triennial election direct that nine members to the School Board shall be elected in accord- ance with the resolution passed by the Board which is hereby approved by the Education Department under section 31 (1) of the Elementary Education Act,lB7o. The Chairman said they could only express their regret that they had not the advantage of nine members. It was rather a peculiar coincidence that when the Board was in a position to adopt nine the majority of the members voted against, but now that the members were all in favour of it it could not be adopted.—Dr. Gastaldi thought they might ask the Education Department to reconsider the question inasmuch as the matter was under consideration before the election.—Mr. Imison thought it was only fair to state on behalf of those who voted against the increase of members in the first in- stance, that they did not know there Was a general desire for the increase. They assumed that there was no evidence of strong feeling on the part of the public, and he thought they were rather entitled to credit inas- much as they acquiesced in it when they knew there was a desire for it, and tried to brine it about, IaBsIGNATION OF TBACHERs.

Letters resigning their positions were read from Mr. George Rowley, Brunswick-st. boys' school, and Miss Mayan, Greenway-road Board (boys') school. In the case of the former teacher the usual term of notice was relinquished inasmuch as he was desirous of departing almost immediately for South Africa.

APPLICATIONS FOR INCREASED SALARIES.

The following applied for increase of salaries :—Miss Jessie Ashbrook, Granville-st. school ; Miss Ellen Crow- ther, Granville-st. sctlool ; Mr. J. C. Cragg, Greenway- road boys' school; Mr. J. Pritchard, caretaker at the Greenwav-road schools;and Mr.J.F Moulton,head master of the Greenway-road school.who stated that he respect- fully renewed the application he made sixteen months ago for an increase of salary. It was n)w upwards of five years since he received an advance, and he confi- dently trusted that the series of excellent examination results and annual reports of H.M.I. had given them perfect satisfaction and they would, therefore,be willing that the renumeration of his services should be equal to that of the headmasters under similar circumstances. He begged, respectfully, therefore, to suggest that his present quite inadequate stipend £lBO be raised to £250 per annum.—The letters ware referred to the new Board, on the motion of Dr. McDougall,seconded by Mr. F. J. Norman.—Dr. McDougall gave notice that he would move at the next meeting that all correspondence should be received by the Clerk, 24 hours before the meeting ef the Board.

THE CHAIRMAN THANKED FOR HIS SERVICES.

I Dr. McDougall said before Mr. Pritchard left the chair he would like to propose a vote of thanks to him for the way he had discharged his duties. Although he (Dr. McDougall)had not always been in sympathy with his rul- ing,yet he had always recognised that the Chairman had been anxious to discharge his duties as fairly as possible. 'raking a retrospect of the last three years he was sorry that they had not been able to discharge their duties in a more expeditious manner than they had been able to do. The transfer of the two denominational schools en- tailed considerable anxiety and time. He hoped that as these schools had been transferred and were working well to the advantage of the public,that the Board would see its way to carry out in a more energetic manner the policy en undated by the Board than they had hitherto done. It was exceedingly unfortunate that the plans for the new schools in Greenway-road which had been before the Board, had been kicked about from pillar to post. They had been sent from the Committee to the Board and from the Board to the Committee, and at present there were no plans before the Board. It was evident that every year the Board continued to hire the schools, it was a yearly loss to the public. The amount paid yearly for them would, if capitalised, be sufficient for the new schools it was proposed to erect. It was very evident that if the next Board members intended to acquit them- selves as business men, they must adopt business habits and capacity, and combine in having the plans passed quickly and starting the building operations at the pro- per time, viz., as the spring opened. He thought the Board wbuld not be doing itself justice without passing that vote of thanks. The remarks he had made were not a reflection on the chairman because the Board must assume the responsibility of their own acts. Mr. Imison said he would like to associate himself with the testimony regarding the chairman,and seconded the proposition. The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi said Dr. McDougall seemed to cast a little discredit en the Beard for its action with reference to the pans connected with the new buildings. But he thought if Dr. McDougall examined his own con- science he would find that ha had been the chief oppon- ent against the wish of the majority of the Board. He had wanted them to build baths, caretaker's rooms, Board rooms, and other things, involving the ratepayers in an expenditure of £B,OOO or £7,000. The Board was not prepared to do all this, but the members were satis- fied that good substantial schools chould be provided for the education of the children, and on that point every member was with Dr. McDougall. With regard to the remarks which had been made respecting the chairman, they were in perfect unison with his own feelings. Mr. F. J. Norman said his short experience on the Board fully entitled him to endorse what had been said respecting the Chairman. The Chairman, in reviewing what had taken place during the last triennial term of the Blard. said that with the attendance of scholars, they had arrived at the highest point of averages-83 per cent. Unfortunately they had lost one of their colleagues, much t the regret of every member of the Board. With regard to Dr. McDougall's objection regarding the new schools, he thought Dr. Gastaldi had rightly stated that none else but Dr. McDougall should be held respon- sible. _ . .

Dr. McDougall: It is not trite. In support of his con- tention be passed in review the proceedings of the Board both in committee and in the Board,and considered that Dr. Gastaldi was not free from blame. He (Dr. Mc- Dougall's) was a definite policy, whilst that of the Board was a negative one, or no policy at all. The Chairman said the majority of the Board could not see their way to accept the plans. Dr. McDougall pointed out that it was two months since the plans were referred back to the committee and they had not been touched since.

The Chairman continuing said he would like te remind the members that the Board had attained its majority. It was just 21 years since the Board was formed and he (the chairman) had been elected at seven triennial elections. He referred to the kind of scholars who first attended the Board schools,and contrasted them with tbe present, and he tendered his thanks to the teachers for bringing the schools into their present high state of efficiency. He was very pleased to be in the positien to make mention of such satisfactory results. The Board then rose.

The surrounding property was depreciated, and he himself had lost an excellent • tenant of his house which meant a loss wf £lOO per annum to him. He, urged that the Rural Council take prompt action to have the nuisance removed, as it was a most serious one. At the same time he had no wish. to injure any busines interests connected with Frodsharn and divert industry from the neighbourhood.—Mr. Dar bysiiire proposed that the nuisance inspector visit the place asd see whether 3, nuisance existed detrimental to health, and if so he proposed that the railway company be served with a notice to abate it.—Mr. Aaron thought that as the sub- ject was so serious that some steps should he taken te , abate it.—Mr.Williams stated that the Frocisham Parish Council had taken up the matter repeatedly and Mr. Thorne had visited the place.lf he was rightly informed the siding that would he necessary to carry the material to the works would cost £3OOO. He believed Mr.•Thorne had brought the subject befere the Joint Committee. The nuisance was greatest in hot weatter.—lt was decided that the Clerk should forward the communication to Mr. Thorne,' asking the railway company to take steps for the abatement of the nuisance.

TAR ROAD ACROSS TIII3 LOCKS.

Mr. William Long, chairman of the Grappenhall Parish Council, wrote with respect to Latchford locks, stating that the council was of the opinion that the obligation of the Manchester Ship Canal Company to provide suitable and sufficient protection to the public crossing these locks was not carried out, as 'instanced by the late melancholy loss of life since they called the attention of the Rural 'Council to the matter. It trusted that the Council would press for the immediate attention of the canal cPmpany and would respectfully support a meeting on the spot of representa- tives of the Rural Council and the canal auth- orities to which the Parish Council would send a deputation, arid that no time might be lost in putting an end to the existing dangers.—The Clerk said the sec- tion of the Act of Parliament stated that the company must maintain the footpath across the locks for ever,and must provide suitable rails.—Mr. Baxter proposed that the Clerk write to the Ship CAnal Compsny drawing their attention to the section of the Act of Parliament and to the fact that no rails had been provided.—Mr. Swinton stated that there was R 3 well defined path. Sometimes the people had to cross over one lock and sometimes over the other, just as they were open or closed. The Council ought to get a defined road.—Mr. Aaron second id the motion, which was carried. THE SHIT"' CANAL BILL, LIGHT RAILWAYS, &C.

The Clerk read the report of the Parliamentary Com- mittee. With regard to the Bill of the Manchester Ship Company,it was proposed by Mr. Hey wood,seconded by Mr. Chadwick, and resolved that steps be taken to pro- tect the authority's works. It was also resolved that a petition be lodged against the Bill,and that the Council's surveyors and inspector be instructed to prepare details, showing the levels and low water mark of all the outfalls of the sewers belonging tc the Authority emptying into the canal,and also showinp* how such sewers will be affected by the raising of the waters of the caaal and the river Weaver. It was further resolved that the sur- veyor and inspector be paid extra for the work to be ex- ecuted. With reference to the question of light railways, it was resolved that the committee were in favour of the establishment of light railwa.ys,and that it Was desirable there should be state and local aid in their construction. The committee were of the opinion that if feasi- ble, the railways should be so constructed that the necessity of unloading and reload- ing* on the main lines be avoided The Par- liamentary Committee also resolved with regard to the new bill of the London aryl North Western Rail- way that the Clerk obtain a copy of the bill and that the surveyor examine the deposited plans and report to thw next meeting.—These minutes were approved on the motion of Mr. Heywood, seconded by Mr. Woodward.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

The monthly meeting of the above Council was held at the Ea:lestown Town Hall on Monday afternoon. Mr. C. R. F. Borron, J.P.,C.C., occupying the chair. There were also present, Messrs. J. W. Emmett, T. Ormand,E. T. Evans, %V. Beatty, P. McMahon, W. Gilmour, R. Stone. J. Simpson, J. Slee, W. Thompson jun., J. Smith, F. J. Wood, Rev. E.W.%V.Kaye,J.K.Worsley (law clerk). TIIE TOTE OF CENSURE ON MR. MACMAFIONT RESCINDED.

The minutes of the last monthly meetiag which con- tained the reourd of a vote of censure upon Mr. Mc- Mahon for certain language he had used with reference to the law clerk, were moved by Mr. R.. Stone, seconded by Mr. Thompson.—Mr. J. W. Emmett said that he had something to say about the minutes, and before he did so, he would wish ail the Council a happy and prosperous Yew Year, and he hoped that after the Christmas holi- days they nad all come with a determination to carry out and facilitate the business of the present year to the best of their ability. (Hear, hear. He thought they could not do better at that meeting than to strike out the minute referring to the vote of censure upon Mr. MacMalion. He hoped and fruited that they were all in a good humour and would allow that minute to be ex- punged entirely outof the minute book. It would be a great pity to allow it to go forwari. Fie thought that Mr. 'McMahon had perhaps made a slip ofthe tongue. Ile did not know whether it was intentional, but he did not think so. He had not spoken to Mr. McMahon about the matter, and he had taken it entirely upon himself to ask the Council to expunge that minute from the minute book. Mr. McMahon was an old commissioner and that was the first time hehad ever had a censure passed upon He kMr. Erminett) did not think that Mr. McMahon had any intention of insulting the law clerk or anybody else. He therefore proposed his motion that the minute be expunged from the book.—The Chairman hoped that the council would conduct the business and do the work with less talk in the coming year. (Hear, hear) With regard to the proposition of Mr. Emmett, he (the chairman) had given Mr. McMahon an , opportunity of withdrawing his expression at the last 1 meeting. He thought that it would be better before , they dealt with the matter at alt, to hear the law clerk and Mr. McMahon.—Mr.Ormand said that in order that the matter might be put right, he had very much pleasure in seconding the resolution of Mr. Emmett. He thought that the minute would be a lasting disgrace to the Council,and it it remained it would not conduce in any shape or form to the dignity of the Council. It was perfectly true that Mr. McMahon was censured at the last meeting for some remarks he made, but he thought that they ought not to commence the new year by rati- fying that vote of censure by putting it upon the min- utiae book for all time against Mr. McMahon. He hoped and trusted that the members of the Council would pass the motion.—Mr. Stone,as the mover of the vote of cen- sure expressed his willingness to have the minute ex- punved.—Mr. MeMahon was then a.eked to say a few words. tie said that he never intended to say that Mr. Vforsley would not record the names correctly and more- over averred that mistakes had occurred with reference to his name,but he did not blame Mr. Worsley for it. The Chairman : I understand that you desire to throw no slur upon Mr. Worsley.—Mr. McMahon: Not the slightest.—Mr.Worsley after detailing the circumstances which led to the passing of the censure, said that he thanked the Board most sincerely for defending him There would be no one more pleased than he was that the motion should now be withdrawn. He had not the .lightest feeling against Mr. McMahon. He thought that the Council knew him well enough for that. He thought that the matter had been sufficiently noticed by the Council.—The Chairman having briefly spoken in favour of the motion, it was carried unanimously.

OAS COMMITTEE.

The minutes which we published last week were Pro- posed by Mr. Smith, seconded by Mr. Ormand.—Mr. McMahen said that he wanted to !Tier to the gas mains in Common-road. He found that they were laid on the side of the road opposite to the houses. It was unreason- able that the gas should be so far from the houses. He did not wish to propose a vote of censure on the gas engineer—(laughter)—but as the land on one side of the road was common land, there would be no houses built there, he thought that it was not right to put the gas mains so far away from the houses. They had to cut the streets across in so many places to get the gas to the houses, and it thus added to the expense to which the property owners were put.—Mr. Beatty said that on the opposite side of the road, the water pipes ware laid, and it was usual to lay the water pipes on one side of the road, and the gas pipes on the other.—Mr. Gilmour thanked Mr. McMahon, who represented Viaduct ward, for taking so much interest in a street which was in the Town Hall ward of which he (Mr. Gilmour) was the representative. He was of the same opision as Mr. McMahon,that the pipes were placed too far away from the houses.—Mr. Emmett also spoke in the same strain. —Mr. Smith, in reply, said that before these strictures were made, they ought to have the gas engineer present. lie would, no doubt, satisfy them as to the reason he acted as he did. He would advise that the questions be asked in committee. He (Mr. Smith) had been on Gas Committees for over 30 years, ard he always found that lay men could always make gas better than the gas engineer, and that they could conduct gas works better than the chairman of the Gas Committee. (Laughter.) He, however, hoped that the questions would be put in committee.—The minutes then passed.

FINANCE COMMITTEE.—TUE ACCOUNT FOR TECIINICAL ItaTRUCTION.—ACRIMONIOUS DISCUS.ION.

Mr. Ormand in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Finance Committee said that among the accounts to be passed was the unfortunate cheque about which inquiries were Made at the last meeting. It was not brought before the last meeting because Mr. Brierley was not present. Mr. Brierley had told him that he had ' sent it to the Viaduct Instruction Committee simply to save the bank commission upon the amount. He had sent the cheque forward,instead of bringing it before the Finance Committee. In future such an occurrence would not be repeated. He thonght that the chairman had some remarks to make relative to this, as his (Mr. Ormand's) name was perhaps inadvertently introduced into the debate a month ago.—The Chairman said that he had no observations to make. He thought that the matter had been properly brought up by the chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee, as he was ask- ing for information with regard to the cheque. It was quite right that the question asked at the last meeting should be put, and he was only sorry that the chairman of the Finance Committee could not see his way at once to state that some irregularity had taken place. He had now made an explanation which was very satisfactory. —Mr. McMahon seconded the Finance Committee's minutes. At the same time, he thought that Mr. Borron should apologise to the chairman of the Finance Com- mittee. It was only fair that he should do so, as many ratepayers had been asking what had been done with the money. It was only fair to Mr. Ormand that an apology should be made to him.—Mr. Gilmour said that Mr. Mrron distinctly told them in committee that he would withdraw and apoloaise to Mr. Ormand for what he had said. As he had preferred to apologise in committee,he ought to apologise in the Council as the matter had been the talk of all the town. The Rev.E.W. W. Kaye ought also to withdraw.—The Chairman said that what he had said was that if any cause arose he should certainly he happy to apologise for the remarks he had made. The public must judge, a 3 the Chairman of the Finance Committee had said that the matter would no occur again in future. Mr. Kaye had asked a ques- tion, and it had now been admitted that the cheque was endorsed and received by the Viaduct Institute.—Mr. Ormand : It was not endorsed by me. It was endorsed by the clerk.—The Chairman I It was endorsed by the clerk to " T. Ormand."—Mr. Ormand : I wish you would di tinguish between my private and public capa- city. I do not refer to you as a glass bottle manufac- turer.and I thank you to keep my private affairs out of the Council.—The Chairman : I am not addressing you as a clerk to the railway at all.—Mr.Ormand : You are referring to me in my private capacity.—The Chairman again contended that the cheque was endorsed by Mr. Brierley to the Chairman of the Finance Comnaittee, and that that cheque did not appear tefore the Council until that day. He therefore did not think that he had done or said anything which he should withdraw. The Chair- man of the Finance Committee was supposed to exercise supervision over money matters which came to that Council. It was his duty to see that those matteri came before the Council.—Mr. McMahon : If the County Council Technical Instruction Committee sent money directed to me, is the chairman of the Finance Commit- tee responsible for that ? The cheque came to our clerk and survevor.—The Chairman : As representing the Council.—Mr.MacMahon : Is the chairman to follow our clerk when he goes away ill, even when he does not know whether there is a cheque or not ?—Mr. Orman(' denied that he ought to have answered the questions at the last meeting. Ho ought to have had not ice given so that he should get information upon them, just as the chairmen of the Gas or Highways Committees were al- lowed notice. He was sorry that at the commencement of the new year the Chairman could not see his way to make the apology public which he had made at the last meeting of the Finance Committee.—Mr. Gilmour : You said at that committee meeting that you left'your- self in the hands of Mr.Ormand, and if he requested you you would offer an apology.—Mr. Ormand said that the chairman had said that he was satisfied with the state- ment that he (Mr.Ormand) had made, and he now asked for an apology which was only fair tc him.elf. Mr. Brierley had endorsed the cheque to save the bulk corn- mission.and if there was some slight irregularity he (Mr. Ormand) was not to blame. As Chairman of the Finance Committee, he was prepared to stand by certain mis- takes, but he was certainly not going to be responsible for irregularities any more than it would he fair to make Mr.Collingwond responsible for the misappropriation of funds by nightsoil men,which was a much more serious charge. He therefore asked the Chairman of the Coun- cil, as a gentleman to a*Tz what he had said with regard to himself. The words .nplained of were to the effect that the chairman had said that " he was sorry that Mr. Ormand had not thought fit to state fairly and clearly what had taken place with reference to the

money, but had sheltered himself behind the Viaduct Committee. He must take the responsibility of that course." He (Mr. Ormand) had stated the case to the committee, and the committee were perfectly satisfied, and Mr. Brierley was put right for the future. The chair- manapologised in committee, and yet he now refused to apologise in public.—Mr. Thompson said that there was no question of censure at all in that matter, and he thought that Mr. Kaye had earned the gratituie of the ratepayers by asking the questions he did. Mr.Ormand himself admitted that the thing had now been put right. and the occurrence would not be permitted in future. He was of the opinion that Mr. Borron in no way merited any censure,om the contrary,he merited their gratitude. —The Rev. E. W.W. Kaye said that he would be pleased to apologise for anything which might have misled the public in reference to Mr. Ormand's character. He did not think that he had mentioned Mr.Ormand's narne,but what he brought forward was that some irregularity had taken place. He mentioned that the chairman of the Finance Committee had brought forward a resolu- tion that no moneys were to be paid from the Council unless with the sanction of the Council. He found that that had been done and therefore he thought that it was his business to bring it forward. He had not mentioned Mr. Ormand's name in connection with the matter simply because he was acting in another capacity. If Mr. Ormand required it he would be pleased to apologise if he had said anything which might have misled the public in reference to the cheque. Mr. Ormand had satisfactorily explained the matter at the last cGmmittee meeting.—Mr. Ormand said that he accepted the apology in the spirit it was given.—Mr. Emmett said that he was surprised that Mr. Kaye had brought the matter forward as he must have known that Mr. Brierley was away from home at the time, and therefore nothing could be done. He also added his testimony to the others by saying that Mr. Borron had said that he would apologise publicly.—The Chairman again denied that he had said that he would apologise unless there was something to apologise for.—Mr. Gilmour: I say again that at the last meeting you said that if Mr. Ormand would ask you to apologise in pub- lic, you would do so.—The Chairman: I said nothing of the kind.—Mr. Gilmour : You promised to withdraw or apologise.—The Chairman : I did not.—Mr. Gilmour : I say you did.—Mr. Simpson : I say he did not.—Mr. Smith : I think it is quite time to close the subject.— After a few more words ea the subject, the minutes of the Finance Committee were put and carried.

THE LETTING OF PUBLIC ROOMS.

Mr. Evans moved that the present scale of charges for letting the Assembly and other rooms in the Town Hall at Earlestown be, and is hereby altered, so as to allow of a reduction of twenty-live per cent. off the present rates being made in the case ot the rooms being taken by ratepayers of the district.—The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye seconded.—Mr. Gilmour moved,and Mr. Smith seconded, that it be referred to the committee, and Mr. Evans hav- ing agreed, this course was adopted. _ _ _ . _ .

This concluded the business. ST. HELENS THEATRE ROYAL.

" CHARLEY'S AUNT."

The popular comedy of " Charley's Aunt "has been produced at St. Helens Theatre Royal this week, and,as on the occasion of its last presentation crowded houses have assembled nightly. The intensely comical situa- tions arising out of the love escapades of three college chums—one of whom masquerades as "Charley's Aunt" —have kept the audiences in a roar of laughter from the rise of the curtain to its fall. Mr.E.H.Wynne as "Lord Fancourt Babberley " (" Charley's Aunt,") has been immensely successful. Mr. Heury Crisp was a capital personator of " Stephen Spettigue,and Mr. Gerald Hoole as "Jack Chesney," and Mr. Herbert Colville as "Charles Wykeham," also appeared to advantage. The characters of" Donna Lucia," " Amy Spettigue," "Kitty Verdun" and 66 Ella Delahv " in the hands of Miss Helen Cress- well, Miss Leila Russell, Miss Muriel Johnston and Miss Janet Le Gros were everything that could be desired. TURNER'S ENGLISH OPER& COMPANY, sr. HELENS.

Om reference to our advertising columns it will be seen that Mr. Wallace Revill, the esteemed proprietor of the St. Helens Theatre Royal, has arranged for a visit of Mr. J. W. Turner, the celebrated tenor, and his English Opera Company next week. They will appear in a round of the hest and most popular operas,and notwith- standing the great attraction and the expensive engage- ment, there will be no advance in the prices of admis- sion. It is to be hoped that the public of St. Helens and the surrounding district will rally round Mr. Revill in his laudable effort to provide such high class enter- tainments for the people. A list of the engagements appears in an advertisement in another column. MRS. LONGSHORE-POTTS AT ST. HELENS.

The visit to St. Helens of Mrs. Longshore-Potts, M.D., of the Women's Medical College of Philadelphia, appears to have created more than usual stir, seeing that the Theatre Royal, was yesterday afternoon crowded with members of the fair sex,when the lady delivered the first of her series of physiological lectures to women. For the purpose of illustrating the lectures, a huge white sheet, 25 feet square, had been fixed on the stage ; com- pletely filling in the proxenium, and upon this numerous superb views of art,anatomy,and scenery were'presented. Mrs. Dr. Potts was heartily received upon making her appearance, The famous lady doctor is a little below the medium height,and is a living testimony to the value of her own theories, being the picture of physical and intellectual health, and not having had an ache or a pain for many years. In opening the proceedings, Mrs. Dr. Potts said that such a large gathering as had there as- sembled indicated a keen interest in themselves and the welfare of women generally. Woman, she said, often shunmed the knowledge that was peculiarly important to her—the knowledge of herself—and the result was just as often endless misery and ills and evils, which a better acquaintance with physiology would have taught her to avoid. Innocence should by no means be con- founded with ignorance, and knowledge, it properly given and received, imparted the power to resist temp- tation. Woman was God-made, and she should not be ashamed to thoroughly understand the work of her Crea- tor; vet every day saw the laws physiology outraged, and i girl would kneel to ask her Maker fcr health and strength, while at the same time she distorted Nis work- manship by compressing her body in stays,and by wearing garments of a palpably irrational kind. A woman, she said, might perhaps wear corsets when she was 50— certainly not before. At school a girl should acquire a knowledge of her body as well as of mental qualities ; at home her mother should complete her education by im- pressing upon her the fact of her womanhood and all that the word implied. The Lecturer spoke without notes and with much eloquence. Her language was graceful and refined, her method entirely devoid of indelicacy or offence,and in plain and practical language she unfolded truths which obviously it is good for every woman and girl to know. This afternoon the second lecture of the series will be delivered. Each lecture is different in sub- ject matter, and is illustrated by special limelight views, and all are free except the concluding discourse on " Maternity," next Tuesday.

SHOCKING NEGLECT OF CHILDREN AT EIINCO RN.

THE FATHER SENT TO PRISON.

Benjamin Moores, labourer, Mason•street,was charged at Runcorn Police Court on Monday morning with neglecting his five children under the age of 16 years in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to their health.—Defendant pleaded not guilty.— P.C. Gregory stated that on the 9th of last month he visited the defendant's house at about 10.15 a.m. He found the children had very little clothing on ; there was no fire in the gate, and no food in the heuse. He asked Mrs. Moores where her husband was and she replied that she did not know as he had not been home during the night. Witness again visited the house at 12 noon on the 23rd ult and found all the children except one, in bed. He looked through the house and there was neither fire nor meat in the house. The same night he found the defendant in a lodging-house in Fryer-street. On the 28th ult he paid a further visit and found the children and mother in a most pitiable condition. About midnight Mrs. Moores came to him and asked whether he had seen her husband, and stated that he had not been home for the last two nigh's. Witness visited the common lodging-house in Fryer-street and found the defendant there, and he said he would not take another b—penny home. Witness told him to put on his clothes and go to his wife and child- ren who were waiting for him in the street. It was then after midnight. Witness had warned the defendant several times.—lnspector McAteer, N.S.P.C.C., stated that he had known the defendant for the past eighteen months. He had visited the house on many occasions to see the children and the wife. He found them in a state of destitution, insufficiently clothed, and in a very dirty state. He had prosecuted the defendant for neglect of children,and the magistrates sent him to prison for three months. When he dame out witness advised him to look after the children, but he was a lazy good-for-nothing fellow. He had earned 15s. a week from the Urban Authority, and had only allowed his wife a very small portion of it.—Mr. Wright stated that the house of the defendants was one of the dirtiest in the town. He had only received 25s.in rent for the past six months.—Mrs. Moores said it was over six months since she received 109. in one week from her husband. She did rot want to go to the workhouse. —The defendant was committed to prison for three months, and his wife was advised to apply to the relieving officer.

FARM FIRE AT APPLETON.

A LABOURER CHARGED WI NI INCENDIARISM.

On Tuesday morning at the farm buildings of Mr. James Britch, Appleton Thorn Inn, a fire broke out with disastrous results: The buildings, which front the inn, comprise smithy, stable, shippon, and large shed at the south side. Everything was safe shortly before seven, but soon after smoke was seen issuing from the build- ing by Mr. Britch'a son. A special messenger was des- patched on horse-back to Wilderspool for the fire engine, and it arrived at the scene soon after eight o'clock. Water was with difficulty found some distance awar,and with the combined help of th villagers and the firemen the flames were got under soon after nine o'clock, but net before the whole ef the roof of the shippon and floors had been burnt. The building was stocked with special Scotch seed potatoes. These were all destroyed, but the implements. Szc., were all saved. There were fortunately no cattle in at the time. The amount of the damage was about £2OO. This is covered by insurance in the Manchester Fire Office.

On Wednesday morning, before Mr. James Tinsley, at the Warrington Occasional Court,a farm labourer named James Corfield was charged in custody with having on the 7th inst., at Appleton Thorn, set fire to a certain building in the possession of James Britch with intent to injure him. P.S. Gosling, ot Stockton Heath, said that at about a quarter past eight on the previous morning he received information of a fire at Appleton Thorn. Aceompanied by P.C. Brew he went to the farm of Mr. James Britch at Appleton,and found a shippon on fire. Certain state- ments were made to him showing that the prisoner was suspected of having slept there the previous night. They hunted for him, and ev-ntually P.C. Brew apprehended him whilst in charge of a threshing machine belonging to Mr. Lambert,his employer. The officer told him that he was suspected ot having slept in the shippon on Monday night,aud asked it it was true,to which prisoner replied that he haa slept there, and that he had gone there whilst drunk. Corfield further informed the con- stable that he had been awakened by a man kicking him on Tuesday morning. The man ,afterwards went eut,and prisoner got up. As it was very dark in the shippon he had to light some matches to find his way out, but when he got outside it was daylight. Continu- ing, the Sergeant said that soon after the prisoner left the building some passers-by saw it on fire and gave information of the occurrence.

Edwin Britch, farmer, of Appleton Thorn, son of the owner of the buildings, stated that it was his custom every night to look over his father's buildings before re- tiring to bed. On Monday night he followed this rule, and when approaching the shippen he thought he heard a vaice very much like the prisoner's. He went round to the back, and finding one of the doors slightly open, entered the building. He could not see anyone about,so concluded that all was right and went away. He did net think that the fire had been caused intentionally; he thought it was an ancident.—P. C. Brew spoke to arrest- ing the prisoner, and gave similar evidence to that ot Sergeant Gosling. The prisoner had told him that the matches he had struck he had put out afterwards, and showed witness the manner in which he had extinguished the matches.—Mr. Tinsley observed that it was a very serious charge, and the magistrates were endeav- ouring to put down this practice of sleeping in such buildings as this. He thought the case had better be remanded for further evidence.—This course was decided upon, and the prisoner was remanded until Thursday. On Thursday morning prisoner was again brought before Mr. Tinsley.—William Hankinson, labourer, said on the night in question he was in the Inn. Defendant was there drunk, and he was refused more drink. He was in the place three hours and received no drink while witness was there.—Martin Kevekin, threshing-machine hand, said that on the 6th inst. he went into the public- house with prisoner. Both were drunk and they went out together, and as prisoner fell down and witness could not raise him he left him in the care of some men. —Mr. Tinsley addressing prisoner, said it was a very serious matter, but he did not think he could do any- thing with him. There was no evidence of malicious inteat though he felt strongly in his own mind that pri- soner had caused the damage. The only thing tha'.. could be proved was negligence and there was no punishment for that.—Prisoner was accordingly dis- charged.

ST. ANN'S PARISH CHURCH WEEKLY BIBLE CLASS.

In connection with the above class there was an illustrated lantern lecture given by the Rev. J. Davies on Wednesday evening in the schools, Dan- nett-et. There was a very good attendance. The subject chosen was, " The Pilgrim's Progress." The Lecturer prefaced his remarks by giving a short but interesting sketch of the life of the author,John Bunyan. The slides thrown upon the sheet illus- trated the most well known scenes in the book, the rev, gentleman carefully explaining each, and also giving the connection between the different views. But in addition to the interest which was taken in the illustrations of this well known allegory, profit was combined, for from each scene was drawn and pressed home practical lessons. Owing to the diffi- culty of treating such. a large and interesting sub- ject in one evening, it is hoped to continue the subject next Wednesday evening at the usual hour 7.30, and at the same .place. The meeting opened and closed by the singing of a hymn and prayer. NARROW ESCAPE FROM DROWNING.—PLUCKY CON- DUCT OF A YOUNG WOMAN.

An accident occurred on Saturday afternoon, at the branch of the old river behind the houses in Wilderspool:causeway, which fortunately had no serious termination,but which it is to be hoped will be a warning to young boys of the danger which they expose themselves to,by playing on the baulks of timber which are kept in the water at that place. At about three o'clock, a boy named Shaw, from Brick-st., was playing on one of the baulks, and at the time, the river was very high, owing to the flow of the tide. The timber was damp and slippery, and the boy fell into the water about three or four feet from the bank. The occurrence was seen by Miss Annie Mooney, domestic servant of Mrs. Blinston, 174, Wilderspool-road, and she rushed to the bank and got on one of the baulks of timber in the hope of getting to the boy. The slippery nature of the timber, however, caused her to lose her foot- ing, and she also fell into the. water, and had great difficulty in getting out .again. Her screams and shouts had in the meantime aroused the attention of a large number of people,and the boy was pulled out of the water not much the worse for his involu- untary immersion. A large number of boys are continually playing and larking upon these baulks of tinaber, and it is a wonder that there are not more accidents of a similar character. Miss .Mooney is to be complimented ppon her plucky action, and she was uLfortunate in completely spoiling her dress.

TAKING COLD. A " Cold " is not nPcessarily the result of a high or ow temperature. A person may go from a bot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrary,he may take cold by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon ainerent parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. •rtl very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is G WILYAI EVAN'S QUININE BITTERS, E VEGETABLE TONIC, Which is the most perfect Vegetable Remedy known ,and the most efficacious to strengthen the constitution, en- liven the drooping or deprested soirits,brace the Nerves, and remove all Disorders of the Stomach which are caused by Indigestion, such as Loss of Appetite, distress alter Eating, Acidity of Stomach,Eructations, Heaviness and Langour. Disorders of the Liver are often caused by defective Digestion. The potency of the gastric juice in the process of Digestion is wonderfully aided by the powerful ingredients contained in GWILYII EVANS' QUININE BITTERS, THE VEGETABLE TONIC. While the Stomach recovers its normal tone, the putrid undigested food is cleared away, and the poor dyspeptic is relieved of his load of misery, once more enjoys good health, and looks forward to happiness and long life.
